<a id="hoffman-plastic-compounds-inc-v-nlrb-merits-2002-03-27"></a>

# Hoffman Plastic Compounds, Inc. v. NLRB; No. 00-1595. — 2002-03-27

[Authoritative Record](../records/hoffman-plastic-compounds-inc-v-nlrb_merits_2002-03-27.md)

## Event

Hoffman Plastic Compounds, Inc. v. NLRB, No. 00-1595. Merits decision. Argued January 15, 2002; decided March 27, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the en banc United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, which enforced the Board's limited backpay order.

Hoffman hired Jose Castro in 1988 after he presented another person's birth certificate and discharged him for union activity in January 1989. He lacked work authorization throughout the relevant period. The established labor violation is outside this review; the question is whether immigration law bars the limited award for unperformed work, as distinct from wages already earned. At the June 14, 1993 compliance hearing, Hoffman learned the disqualification. The Board denied reinstatement and ended the backpay period that day.

The Board and administrative law judge recount present-tense testimony supporting an inference that Castro continued using the borrowed certificate and false identification for work through the hearing. Their recitals identify no named interim employer or dated later tender. The judge's account that the driver's license and Social Security card apparently derived from the certificate is an inference, not a separately reproduced admission of that acquisition sequence. The Board found reasonable interim-work efforts; that finding does not establish how each job was procured. Counsel's proposed lawful-casual-work explanations and alleged Board inducement of fraud are not findings.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the judgment enforcing the Board's limited backpay order.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

Enforcement of the actual Board order remains in place. Its stated $66,951, plus the specified interest and less required federal and state withholding, covers backpay through the last quarter of 1992. January 1 through June 14, 1993 is the separately remaining period under that order; the stated sum is not a final total through discovery. The remaining amount is to be calculated within the Board's existing remedial framework. Reinstatement is denied and no backpay accrues after June 14, 1993 on the premise of continued unauthorized employment. The underlying unfair-labor-practice finding and other lawful relief remain undisturbed. Independent immigration enforcement and sanctions remain available.

## Opinion Topology

STEVENS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and SOUTER, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined. O'CONNOR, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which SCALIA, KENNEDY and THOMAS, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirm enforcement | Complete statutory accommodation, actual award and remaining-period limits, preservation of independent sanctions and absence of an unsupported additional remand |
| Dissenting opinion | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas | Would reverse enforcement of the disputed backpay category | Statutory conflict for unperformed work in employment obtained through fraudulent verification while unauthorized; preserve the labor violation and other lawful relief |

## Holdings

### Limited monetary relief under the two statutes

**Controlling proposition:** IRCA does not categorically withdraw the Board's NLRA §10(c) authority to award limited pre-discovery backpay for an established antiunion discharge, even where the worker lacked employment authorization and obtained the job through false verification documents. The Board may remedy that independent labor violation through a retrospective monetary award that orders no unlawful employment, denies reinstatement and ends the ordinary lost-work remedy at actual discovery of the employment bar; the award supplies neither work authorization nor immunity from separate sanctions.

**Authority:** Stevens's opinion, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Their five votes establish this rule and enforcement of the actual limited order.

**Operative statutory limits:** Section 10(c)'s affirmative remedial authority remains bounded by the NLRA and other applicable enactments. Its separate bar for an actual discharge for cause is not established by a reason discovered only after an antiunion discharge. Section 1324a separately prohibits covered knowing hiring or fee-based recruitment/referral, requires employment verification, and prohibits knowing continued employment of an unauthorized worker. Authorization is employment- and time-specific, turning on permanent-resident status or statutory or Attorney General authorization. Good-faith verification can defend the knowing-hire charge; it does not permit knowing continuation after discovery. No earlier immigration-law violation by Hoffman is adjudicated here.

Section 1546(b) prohibits use, for employment verification, of identification known or reasonably understood not to have been lawfully issued for its possessor, false identification with that knowledge standard, or a false attestation. Its operative prohibition predates the 1988 hiring; the later five-year penalty does not govern the original act. Section 1324c applies only to violations on or after November 29, 1990, not the original tender; a later violation requires its own applicable act, knowledge, purpose and time predicates. Later benefit/application amendments and the 1996 deletion of the birth-certificate document category do not apply retroactively.

Both document statutes retain exceptions for lawfully authorized investigative, protective or intelligence activity of federal, state or state-subdivision law-enforcement agencies or federal intelligence agencies. Section 1324c(b) separately preserves activity authorized by 18 U.S.C. chapter 224; section 1546(c) separately preserves activity authorized by title V of the Organized Crime Control Act of 1970. The latter provision's State definition includes the District of Columbia, commonwealths, territories and possessions. No such activity is implicated here. Neither statute generally criminalizes the worker's mere performance of work.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 10(c) supplies the monetary authority, while IRCA supplies distinct employment, verification and fraud restrictions. Compensation for completed unlawful retaliation does not direct the forbidden future employment. Southern Steamship requires accommodation of another federal prohibition, not abandonment of every monetary labor remedy; this order commands no prohibited hiring or work. Sure-Tan's departed workers and unlawful-reentry problem do not establish a universal bar for the worker who remained here. ABF rejects automatic remedial forfeiture from serious misconduct while preserving actual statutory and remedial limits. McKennon likewise separates the unlawful decision from later-discovered disqualification. Its actual-would-discharge predicate and ordinary pre-discovery measure, subject to extraordinary equitable circumstances, belong to its after-acquired-evidence setting; the analogy does not itself construe IRCA.

The dissent's strongest objection is that replacement wages value an employment opportunity federal law made unavailable and may encourage further fraud. The Court accepts the original fraud and the real continuing-use evidence. Neither mitigation duties nor an award authorize false documentation. But the identified prohibitions do not withdraw this bounded retrospective authority. Denying reinstatement and ending accrual at actual discovery give the employment restriction practical effect; independent sanctions retain their full force. Removing every backpay consequence would also reduce the cost of targeting this workforce for antiunion discharge. That consequence supports the statutory accommodation; deterrence alone supplies no remedial power.

The Board confronted the known disqualification and fraud, found reasonable interim efforts and adopted this limited accommodation. A particular preserved calculation, mitigation or explanation defect remains reviewable on its actual basis. None is demonstrated here merely by disputing the statutory choice or the reach of the continued-use inference. No additional formula, evidentiary hearing or explanation remand is required. The award remains restorative within §10(c), not a general punitive authority or automatic entitlement for every unauthorized worker.

**Application and remaining calculation:** Enforcement covers the order described in Judgment & Remedy, including the remaining January 1–June 14, 1993 period; this decision supplies no new total. Ordinary calculation and mitigation requirements remain operative. The employer's specified initial answers did not challenge the method, but it contested calculations and periods at the March 4, 1993 hearing; the Board found its method valid and reasonable. Its no-exception observation concerned receipt of evidence and original fraudulent acquisition of the Hoffman job, not a newly established tender at a named interim employer. The continuing statutory objection, the administrative contest and the Board's effort finding remain distinct. The Court relies on no blanket waiver and finds neither every interim job lawful nor every job fraudulently obtained.

## Precedent Treatment

- ABF Freight System, Inc. v. NLRB is applied to preserve reviewable remedial discretion and reject automatic forfeiture for misconduct. It does not protect relief contrary to another statute, misconduct defeating a remedial predicate, or arbitrary or inadequately explained relief.
- McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co. supplies an analogy separating an unlawful decision from later-known disqualification. Its actual-would-discharge requirement and ordinary pre-discovery backpay measure, subject to extraordinary equitable circumstances, remain intact; its ADEA rule does not decide the NLRA/IRCA interaction.
- Sure-Tan, Inc. v. NLRB retains labor coverage and the duty to accommodate immigration law. Its departure, reentry and legal-availability setting is distinguished from this limited monetary order for a worker who remained here.
- Southern Steamship Co. v. NLRB remains a limit on remedies conflicting with independent federal prohibitions. It does not bar this award, which directs no prohibited employment.
- New York v. FERC / Enron Power Marketing, Inc. v. FERC preserves review of an actual agency-authority or explanation defect. The Board's identified accommodation is the legal choice presented, not an unexplained alternative requiring an additional remand.

## Law After Decision

An established antiunion discharge may carry the limited pre-discovery backpay consequence upheld here despite the employee's lack of authorization and original verification fraud. Labor coverage, the actual employer violation and the legality of a particular remedy remain separate questions. The award must stay within the governing monetary authority and ordinary remedial predicates; it neither guarantees all unauthorized workers backpay nor orders future employment. Immigration verification duties, knowing-employment prohibitions and independently applicable document sanctions remain enforceable. Actual calculation and mitigation objections require their own preserved factual and legal basis; neither blanket forfeiture nor an unsupported new administrative inquiry follows from the statutory dispute.

## Separate Writings

**O'Connor, dissenting, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.** The dissent would reverse enforcement of this backpay category because it replaces earnings from an employment opportunity federal law made unavailable and Castro obtained through fraudulent verification. Section 10(c)'s public remedial purpose does not permit the Board to restore that lost-work premise in conflict with the independent federal employment scheme. The payment need not itself constitute hiring, and IRCA need not expressly repeal backpay, for that remedial conflict to matter.

Southern Steamship requires the Board to respect the independent prohibition. Sure-Tan's legal-availability limit remains significant despite its different departure facts; the dissent's sufficient ground is the operative immigration scheme and the established original fraud and lack of authorization, not that precedent's sentence alone. ABF addressed misconduct that did not render the underlying employment federally unavailable and preserves only lawful remedial discretion. McKennon's later-discovered employer ground does not authorize compensation premised on employment forbidden by another enactment. Its actual-would-discharge requirement therefore does not resolve this different statutory bar.

The discovery cutoff and denial of reinstatement prevent future employment, but do not remove the defect in replacement wages for the earlier unauthorized position. The majority's concern that employers may exploit this workforce is substantial. The established labor violation, cease-and-desist and notice obligations, and other lawful remedies nevertheless cannot supply authority for this conflicting wage-replacement category. Nor can a public deterrence objective alone overcome the remedial limit.

The present-tense testimony supports continued-use concerns, but the dissent needs no finding that every interim job required new false documents, that the Board induced fraud, or that a particular post-1990 document violation occurred. It does not apply section 1324c to the original hiring. Its proposed reversal preserves NLRA coverage, the employer's violation, other lawful relief and independent immigration enforcement. It decides neither earned wages nor every remedy involving an unauthorized worker. It would return only for implementation of the backpay bar, not a new mitigation hearing or an additional punitive-remedy holding.

## Procedure After Action

The en banc judgment enforcing the limited Board order is affirmed. The $66,951 through the last quarter of 1992 remains payable with the order's specified interest and required withholding. Calculation of the separately remaining January 1–June 14, 1993 period proceeds within the existing Board framework, subject to applicable ordinary remedial requirements. This affirmance orders no additional explanation or evidentiary remand, no reinstatement and no postdiscovery accrual based on unauthorized continued employment. Other lawful labor relief and independent immigration proceedings retain their proper routes.

## Source Notes

No. 00-1595; 535 U.S. 137; NLRA §10(c), 29 U.S.C. §160(c); 8 U.S.C. §§1324a and 1324c; 18 U.S.C. §1546(b)–(c). Board order and accounting qualification, Pet. App. 85a–86a & n.12; testimony recitals and evidentiary qualification, Pet. App. 79a–80a, 84a n.10 and 92a. The stated award covers through the last quarter of 1992, with the period through June 14, 1993 remaining separately. The general testimony supports a continuing-use inference; the administrative recitals identify no particular later document tender. The acquisition sequence for the additional identification documents is the administrative law judge's inference.

---

<a id="mickens-v-taylor-merits-2002-03-27"></a>

# Mickens v. Taylor; No. 00-9285 — 2002-03-27

[Authoritative Record](../records/mickens_v_taylor_merits_2002-03-27.md)

## Event

Mickens v. Taylor, No. 00-9285. Argued November 5, 2001; decided March 27, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the en banc Fourth Circuit, reviewing denial of federal habeas relief. The appointed lawyer had recently represented the murder victim, and the appointing judge knew the relationship but did not inquire or disclose it. The question is whether that omitted inquiry dispenses with a showing that the conflict adversely affected representation. A federal evidentiary hearing resulted in a finding against adverse effect; the separate factual challenge to that finding is outside the granted question.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The denial of habeas relief on the reviewed conflict claim is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

**Opposing:** Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, who would reverse and obtain a new trial on the separate grounds stated below.

The conviction and sentence remain undisturbed on this claim. The Court orders no new trial or additional conflict hearing solely because the judge failed to inquire.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Adverse-effect requirement, grant limits and affirmance in full |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Disclosure, informed acceptance and exceptional protective-duty ground; new trial |
| Dissent | Souter | None | Prospective judicial-duty ground; new trial; no actual-conflict-only Wood remand |
| Dissent | Breyer | Ginsburg | Narrow State-created structural ground; new trial; general inquiry framework reserved |

## Holdings

### An omitted inquiry does not itself establish the adverse effect necessary here

**Holding and operative rule:** Outside the established circumstances requiring automatic relief, a trial court's failure to inquire into a particular potential conflict it knew or reasonably should have known about does not itself establish a Sixth Amendment violation. A defendant invoking the conflict-based relaxation of ordinary outcome prejudice must at least show that competing obligations adversely affected counsel's performance; that showing is different from proving a reasonable probability of a different verdict under Strickland.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas. Those five directly adopt the rule, the confined application and affirmance. The four dissenters support a new trial on distinct grounds and do not establish a common alternative holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the right protects loyal, effective representation rather than imposing automatic reversal as a sanction for a judge's omitted procedure. Holloway requires automatic relief when counsel makes an adequate timely conflict objection to joint representation and the court compels continuation without the required inquiry or protection. That distinct objection-and-compulsion predicate is absent here. Sullivan requires an actual conflict adversely affecting performance before ordinary outcome prejudice is dispensed with. Wood's actual-conflict inquiry does not create a separate rule awarding relief for divided obligations without an effect on representation in this setting. Cronic's exceptional denial-of-assistance principle does not convert this omitted inquiry into a complete loss of counsel.

The contrary positions emphasize that the State selected the murder victim's recent lawyer, the judge knew the particular risk and Mickens lacked the information needed to object. The Court accepts the seriousness of that combination and the duty to inquire. Swidler preserves confidentiality after death, so death does not eliminate the potential conflict. But neither continuing duties nor judicial silence establishes that counsel withheld a defense, altered a choice or otherwise performed differently. The appointment's gravity alone does not establish the structural denial or representation injury needed for the proposed categorical remedy.

The federal court conducted an evidentiary hearing and found no adverse effect. The granted question asks whether that requirement can be dispensed with; it does not reopen the separate factual challenge to the finding. Even assuming Sullivan's relaxed-prejudice route applies to this successive representation, the existing determination defeats this claim. The Court does not decide its application to every category of successive representation, which lies beyond the reviewed question. Holloway retains its established rule, and no ordinary outcome-prejudice burden is added after the predicates of an applicable conflict exception are satisfied.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Holloway v. Arkansas, 435 U.S. 475:** Preserved for compelled joint representation over an adequate timely objection; its automatic remedy is not extended solely because the judge knew a potential conflict.
- **Cuyler v. Sullivan, 446 U.S. 335:** Applied on the assumption favorable to Mickens that its relaxed-prejudice route is available; an actual conflict adversely affecting performance remains necessary here, without additional ordinary outcome prejudice.
- **Wood v. Georgia, 450 U.S. 261:** Its inquiry obligation and actual-conflict requirement do not establish the proposed separate exemption from adverse effect in this setting.
- **Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668:** Ordinary outcome prejudice remains distinct from the adverse-effect requirement under an applicable conflict exception.
- **United States v. Cronic, 466 U.S. 648:** Its exceptional presumed-prejudice circumstances are not established merely by this unperformed inquiry.
- **Swidler & Berlin v. United States:** Post-death confidentiality remains; its survival proves neither a particular confidence nor impairment of this defense.
- **Both Williams v. Taylor decisions:** Constitutional entitlement, concealed-fact cause, factual development and state-merits review remain distinct; the federal hearing does not supply a state merits adjudication.

## Law After Decision

A known potential conflict and the judge's neglected inquiry do not themselves establish the adverse effect required for this conflict-based exception to ordinary prejudice. Holloway retains its distinct automatic-relief rule. The Court assumes Sullivan's route is available here without deciding its application to every successive representation. The existing no-adverse-effect determination defeats this claim within the granted question.

## Separate Writings

**Stevens, dissenting.** Stevens would reverse the habeas denial and secure a new trial because the State appointed the murder victim's recent lawyer in a capital case without disclosure or the accused's informed acceptance, despite the judge's knowledge. The right to a meaningful lawyer-client relationship, counsel's disclosure obligation and the court's protective duty operate together. This exceptional State-created arrangement warrants presumed impairment under Cronic; Holloway, Sullivan and Wood supply the conflict safeguards, while Swidler preserves continuing confidentiality without proving any particular confidence. His rule concerns this undisclosed capital victim-counsel appointment, not every former-client relationship or ethical breach. He does not require another actual-conflict hearing before recognizing the constitutional entitlement.

**Souter, dissenting.** Souter would reverse and require a new trial under the court's prospective duty to address a sufficiently identified risk before representation unfolds. When the judge already has adequate notice, Holloway's preventive protection should not depend solely on an objection from a potentially conflicted lawyer. The judge must inquire, secure an intelligent waiver or arrange replacement when warranted. Souter distinguishes that prospective duty from later discovery: he reads Wood's retrospective inquiry as requiring actual conflict and adverse effect, rather than supporting an actual-conflict-only remand here. His discussion of possible adverse effects supplies no decision of the factual issue outside the grant and no direction for another hearing.

**Breyer, dissenting, joined by Ginsburg.** These Justices would reverse and secure a new trial on a narrower structural ground. The State supplied the recent criminal lawyer of the murder victim to defend the accused in a capital case; the immediate incompatibility, the capital proceeding's use of the victim's character and the State's creation of the arrangement produce a breakdown in the trial framework. Cronic and Fulminante support relief without reconstructing individual performance effects at that conjunction of facts. Young v. United States ex rel. Vuitton reinforces the concern for institutional fairness. They expressly leave the general relationship among Holloway, Sullivan and Wood unresolved and do not join Souter's general known-risk rule or an actual-conflict-only remand.

Each dissent seeks the new-trial remedy through ordinary conditional habeas relief, permitting the State to retry with constitutionally adequate representation. None orders immediate unconditional release, acquittal or a new sentence alone. Their constitutional positions do not adjudicate an independently presented collateral defense on predicates absent from the question decided. Counsel maintained the narrower actual-conflict theory; the dissenters give their own legal answers to the granted conflict question and the requested new-trial remedy.

## Procedure After Action

The habeas denial stands on the reviewed constitutional ground. The Court does not reopen the factual adverse-effect determination or direct another conflict inquiry. The broader successive-representation question remains outside the grant.

## Source Notes

[Official argument transcript, November 5, 2001](https://www.supremecourt.gov/oral_arguments/argument_transcripts/2001/00-9285.pdf), pp. 23–25 and 55–56, for counsel's actual-conflict limitation and refusal to concede adverse effect. Holloway, 435 U.S. 475; Sullivan, 446 U.S. 335; Wood, 450 U.S. 261; Strickland, 466 U.S. 668; Cronic, 466 U.S. 648. The conflict claim had no state merits adjudication; §2254(d) is not invoked merely from the federal filing date.

<a id="steele-v-hamilton-county-dig-2002-03-27"></a>

# Steele v. Hamilton County Community Mental Health Board; Ohio No. 99-1771 — 2002-03-27

[Authoritative Record](../records/steele_v_hamilton_county_dig_2002-03-27.md)

## Event

Steele v. Hamilton County Community Mental Health Board; Ohio No. 99-1771, 90 Ohio St. 3d 176. Dismissal as improvidently granted, March 27, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Ohio. The question concerns nonemergency involuntary medication of a civilly committed patient who poses no imminent danger, where incapacity, individual benefit and absence of an equally effective less intrusive treatment are established by clear and convincing evidence. Ohio required further patient-specific findings before authorizing medication, but declined remand after voluntary medication was reported.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The writ of certiorari is dismissed as improvidently granted. The unresolved continuing-controversy facts make this writ unsuitable for a federal treatment ruling. No individual vote tally is announced.

## Judgment & Remedy

The Ohio Supreme Court's judgment remains undisturbed without approval of its federal reasoning. The Court orders neither vacatur nor remand. The dismissal supplies no authorization to medicate Steele.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| Procedural dismissal | Per curiam | Unsuitable vehicle; no treatment merits ruling |

## Holdings

The writ is dismissed as improvidently granted because unresolved facts about the continuing controversy make it unsuitable for deciding the federal treatment question. This nonmerits action neither declares the case definitively moot nor endorses the Ohio Supreme Court's treatment rule; its judgment remains undisturbed.

## Precedent Treatment

Steel Co. preserves the bar on assuming disputed Article III jurisdiction to decide contested merits. ASARCO preserves the distinction between state adjudicatory authority and a federal personal stake. Harper and Erie involved established continuing exposure or legal disability, which this record does not establish. Laidlaw's defendant-cessation rule does not automatically govern a patient's acceptance of medication. Bancorp supplies no automatic vacatur; Izumi confirms that dismissal of an unsuitable writ leaves the judgment below without merits approval. No treatment precedent is altered.

## Law After Decision

The federal nonemergency medication question remains unresolved by this action. Ohio's own judgment retains its existing force. Voluntary medication is not declared categorically to moot a treatment controversy.

## Separate Writings

None.

## Procedure After Action

Proceedings in this Court are concluded. A later request for compelled treatment must rest on its own lawful authority and then-existing facts. The last established treatment status is voluntary medication; present custody, renewed refusal, an operative authorization and supported same-patient recurrence likely to evade review are not established. The Court makes no definitive mootness finding.

## Source Notes

[Steele v. Hamilton County Community Mental Health Board, 90 Ohio St. 3d 176, 2000-Ohio-47](https://www.supremecourt.ohio.gov/rod/docs/pdf/0/2000/2000-ohio-47.pdf), paragraphs 7–14, 43–49 and footnote 8. Washington v. Harper, 494 U.S. 210, 218–219; ASARCO Inc. v. Kadish, 490 U.S. 605, 617–624. The state record's absence of proof of recovery or release does not establish the patient's circumstances at this Court's disposition.

<a id="adams-v-florida-power-corp-dig-2002-04-01"></a>

# Adams v. Florida Power Corp.; No. 01-584 — 2002-04-01

[Authoritative Record](../records/adams_v_florida_power_corp_dig_2002-04-01.md)

## Event

Adams v. Florida Power Corp., No. 01-584. Argued March 20, 2002; dismissed as improvidently granted April 1, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit, reviewing its answer to an interlocutory certified question whether disparate-impact liability is available under the Age Discrimination in Employment Act. The district court entered a categorical bar without deciding the factual sufficiency of the asserted impact theory; the separate class ruling is outside the certified appeal.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The writ of certiorari is dismissed as improvidently granted. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela dissents. The Court announces no individual tally beyond that separate writing.

## Judgment & Remedy

The Eleventh Circuit judgment remains undisturbed without approval of its statutory reasoning.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins and scope |
|---|---|---|
| Procedural dismissal | Per curiam | Dismisses the writ without deciding ADEA impact liability |
| Dissent | Stone-Zsela | No joins; would reach the statutory question, reverse and remand |

## Holdings

The writ is dismissed as improvidently granted. No controlling proposition concerning ADEA disparate-impact liability is decided.

## Precedent Treatment

The Court alters no precedent. Hazen Paper's reservation of disparate impact remains; the dissent's proposed statutory rule is not adopted.

## Law After Decision

This dismissal creates no Supreme Court rule accepting or rejecting ADEA disparate-impact liability. The judgment below retains its ordinary force.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela dissents. He would decide the certified statutory question, reverse the categorical bar and remand. The district court actually entered and certified that legal ruling, and neither it nor the circuit majority adopted a finding that the impact allegations were insufficient. The unassessed factual merits therefore do not prevent correcting the legal bar. He reads section 623(a)(2)'s effects-oriented classification language together with section 623(f)(1)'s reasonable-factors-other-than-age provision to permit a claim directed to a specifically identified neutral practice that causes an adverse age effect. The employee must identify that practice and establish causation; aggregate workforce disparity alone is insufficient. A proven reasonable nonage basis may justify the differentiation without showing that the practice was the only possible business choice. Hazen Paper reserved impact and does not foreclose that rule. The 1991 Title VII amendments do not justify importing their entire business-necessity scheme into the ADEA. On remand, the lower courts would assess the actual practice, causation, reasonable-factor justification and procedural requirements; recognizing the theory would not itself restore a class or establish liability. This is his dissenting position, not the Court's statutory holding.

## Procedure After Action

The proceeding in this Court is concluded. The existing lower-court proceedings retain their procedural status.

## Source Notes

[Adams v. Florida Power Corp., 255 F.3d 1322](https://media.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/files/199915306.MAN.pdf); 29 U.S.C. §§623(a)(2), 623(f)(1); 28 U.S.C. §1292(b). Hazen Paper Co. v. Biggins, 507 U.S. 604. The lower concurrence's sufficiency assessment was not adopted as a factual finding by the district court or the circuit majority.

<a id="sao-paulo-v-american-tobacco-summary-2002-04-01"></a>

# Sao Paulo State of the Federative Republic of Brazil v. American Tobacco Co.; No. 01-835 — 2002-04-01

[Authoritative Record](../records/sao_paulo_v_american_tobacco_summary_2002-04-01.md)

## Event

Sao Paulo State of the Federative Republic of Brazil v. American Tobacco Co.; No. 01-835. Summary merits decision, April 1, 2002. On petition for certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit. The question is whether 28 U.S.C. §455(a) requires disqualification because the judge was mistakenly identified as an association's president in a motion to file an amicus brief in similar tobacco litigation, considering the continuing executive-committee role, absence of preparation or approval, and vague awareness of that earlier case. The circuit required recusal by relying on its ruling in related Republic of Panama litigation. Gilboy was the earlier amicus matter.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate in the submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court summarily reverses the recusal judgment and remands the case by a unanimous per curiam opinion.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The disqualification requirement on this asserted ground is removed. The case returns for ordinary further proceedings before the assigned judge, subject to any genuinely different recusal ground on its own supported predicates.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Full statutory rule, factual application and reversal with remand |

## Holdings

### Mistaken attribution and continuing organizational leadership

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 455(a) does not require disqualification where a judge was mistakenly identified as an association's president in a motion to file an amicus brief, remained on its executive committee, took no part in preparing or approving the submission, and was only vaguely aware of the earlier case. The informed observer must weigh that genuine leadership role together with the established limits of participation; on these combined facts, the attribution does not reasonably call impartiality into question.

**Authority:** The per curiam opinion is joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt the same statutory rule, application and reversal with remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the appearance inquiry must use the full facts. Liteky supplies the objective standard: an informed, detached observer considers the relevant circumstances together, without requiring proof of actual bias. Liljeberg likewise requires an informed assessment and keeps the existence of a violation distinct from the remedy for one. The mistaken presidential designation cannot be treated as true, but the judge's continuing executive-committee membership cannot be disregarded either.

Organizational leadership can connect a person to an institutional position even without personal authorship. That is the defendants' strongest argument. Here, however, the established absence of preparation or approval and the judge's vague awareness materially limit what the observer can attribute to the judge. Treating this particular brief's advocacy as the judge's own despite those facts would give the organizational affiliation decisive weight it does not bear in this record. The conclusion rests on the objective circumstances, not the judge's assurance of fairness.

Microsoft applies the same standard to supported outside conduct concerning live merits, credibility and relief. Those undisclosed discussions created a materially different relationship to the litigation. The distinction concerns the actual conduct and context; neither press contact nor intentional wrongdoing is a prerequisite to recusal. This decision creates no general exemption for organizational leaders or for people who did not write a submission. Actual participation, responsibility for an organization's position, financial interests and other supported relationships remain subject to the ordinary appearance standard. The circuit's disqualification ruling therefore cannot stand on this ground.

## Precedent Treatment

- Liteky v. United States, 510 U.S. 540 (March 7, 1994): applies the controlling cumulative, informed-observer standard. The absence of actual bias does not defeat an appearance claim, and no additional intensity requirement governs this outside affiliation.
- Liljeberg v. Health Services Acquisition Corp., 486 U.S. 847 (1988): applies its informed appearance inquiry and preserves the separate remedial assessment after a violation is established. Risks of injustice to the parties, injustice in other cases and harm to public confidence neither create an otherwise absent violation nor excuse an established one.
- United States v. Microsoft Corp. (January 22, 2002): distinguishes its supported undisclosed discussions about the live litigation from the limited connection to advocacy established here. Its objective appearance rule and separate treatment of civil relief remain in force.

## Law After Decision

Section 455(a)'s objective appearance standard remains unchanged. On the established combination of mistaken attribution, continuing committee leadership, no preparation or approval and vague awareness, disqualification is not required. The holding protects neither organizational affiliation categorically nor actual advocacy from ordinary appearance review. Liljeberg's remedial inquiry remains distinct from whether a violation exists.

## Separate Writings

No separate opinion is filed.

## Procedure After Action

The Fifth Circuit's recusal judgment is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the Court's opinion. Ordinary adjudication of the underlying tobacco claims continues under the governing law.

## Source Notes

No. 01-835; 28 U.S.C. §455(a); Liteky v. United States, 510 U.S. 540; Liljeberg v. Health Services Acquisition Corp., 486 U.S. 847; United States v. Microsoft Corp., January 22, 2002. Status-conference transcript 8, App. to Pet. for Cert. 54a, records the continuing executive-committee role, absence of preparation or approval and vague awareness. Organizational membership does not establish undisclosed personal participation in this submission.

---

<a id="venetian-casino-resort-v-local-joint-executive-board-merits-2002-04-01"></a>

# Venetian Casino Resort, L.L.C. v. Local Joint Executive Board of Las Vegas; 257 F.3d 937 — 2002-04-01

[Authoritative Record](../records/venetian_casino_resort_v_local_joint_executive_board_merits_2002-04-01.md)

## Event

Venetian Casino Resort, L.L.C. v. Local Joint Executive Board of Las Vegas. Merits decision, April 1, 2002. Review of the Ninth Circuit judgment, 257 F.3d 937. The question is whether the replacement sidewalk along Las Vegas Boulevard remains a traditional public forum despite private fee ownership, given its continuing public-passage function and the government agreement replacing the former public sidewalk. The casino sought exclusion of union demonstrators from that route.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The Ninth Circuit judgment is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Scalia and Thomas, who would reverse and remand.

The public-forum judgment for this replacement boulevard sidewalk stands. The casino's requested blanket exclusion based solely on private title is rejected; lawful neutral measures addressing actual obstruction, safety and access remain available.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Both holdings and affirmance in full |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Rejects governmental attribution; would reverse and remand without reaching forum classification independently |

## Holdings

### I. The replacement public thoroughfare retains constitutional speech protection

**Holding and operative rule:** A sidewalk deliberately substituted for an existing public street route, maintained under a continuing, enforceable public-passage obligation and integrated into that route remains a traditional public forum for peaceful expression. Private fee ownership does not permit a general speech exclusion from that passage; the holding concerns the replacement route, not all privately owned walkways or customer-access areas.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. These seven directly adopt the route-specific governmental-attribution and traditional-public-forum rule, its application and affirmance.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that constitutional responsibility follows the actual public-route arrangement. Government permitted removal of the former public sidewalk while securing a connected replacement, mandatory unobstructed passage, obligations binding successors, and retained protection against hazards. Marsh supplies the limited principle that private ownership cannot defeat constitutional protection of an established governmental public function. Brentwood and Lebron require actual governmental responsibility, rather than an inference from popularity or accessibility alone. Here the government's direct undertaking to preserve its street route supplies that responsibility before forum classification is considered. Grace then supplies the traditional expressive character of an ordinary street sidewalk.

The private-property reservation and future-dedication clause do not disappear. They preserve title, ordinary maintenance and lawful property interests; the Court does not construe the agreement as a present conveyance of title or decide a taking. The reservation itself is bounded by constitutional limits. The obligation to dedicate a further replacement if this walkway is removed, altered or abandoned protects continuity of passage; it does not transform the current mandatory through-route into an invitation the owner may freely withdraw. Lloyd and Hudgens preserve control of genuinely private commercial property, but this passage continues a displaced public street function. The dissent's proposed distinction between access and speech would let the same government-secured street remain compulsory public passage while losing its traditional expressive protection solely through relocated title. The First Amendment does not permit that result on this arrangement.

### II. Forum protection permits lawful neutral protection of passage and safety

**Holding and operative rule:** The casino may not exclude the peaceful demonstration from this replacement sidewalk solely by invoking private title. Traditional-public-forum standards continue to govern: content-based restrictions require a compelling justification and narrow tailoring, while content-neutral time, place and manner restrictions must be narrowly tailored to a significant governmental interest and leave ample alternative channels; viewpoint discrimination is forbidden.

**Authority:** The same seven-Justice Court opinion adopts this consequence and preserves lawful neutral regulation of actual obstruction, safety and access.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that speech protection and continued pedestrian passage operate together. Perry supplies the governing forum standards; Ward permits genuinely neutral management of time, place and manner without requiring the least restrictive conceivable means, while forbidding burdens substantially broader than necessary. Thomas v. Chicago Park District preserves constrained neutral administration of shared expressive space. Those rules leave room to address actual obstruction and safety; public-forum status does not immunize such conduct. The casino's requested blanket exclusion rests on title and therefore fails at this location. No particular obstruction sanction or casino-interior restriction is presented, so the Court decides neither. The Ninth Circuit's judgment concerning this sidewalk is affirmed.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Marsh v. Alabama, 326 U.S. 501:** Applied to the established public-thoroughfare function; ordinary private commercial property does not thereby become governmental.
- **Brentwood Academy v. Tennessee Secondary School Athletic Association:** Its requirement of actual governmental responsibility is applied to the government's binding replacement-route arrangement; public benefit or association alone remains insufficient.
- **Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp.:** Its rejection of dispositive private labels informs the attribution inquiry. Its special-creation and permanent-majority-appointment rule remains sufficient in its own setting, rather than a mandatory template for every public function.
- **United States v. Grace, 461 U.S. 171:** Supplies ordinary street-sidewalk forum protection after governmental responsibility has been established.
- **Lloyd Corp. v. Tanner, 407 U.S. 551, and Hudgens v. NLRB, 424 U.S. 507:** Distinguished because ordinary private shopping property does not continue a displaced government-secured public street route.
- **Perry Education Association v. Perry Local Educators' Association, 460 U.S. 37, and Ward v. Rock Against Racism, 491 U.S. 781:** Their traditional-forum and neutral time, place and manner standards remain governing.
- **Thomas v. Chicago Park District:** Preserves lawful, constrained neutral administration; no particular permit rule is adjudicated here.
- **The Lee companion decisions:** Supply no controlling airport classification or combined rationale; their fractured judgments are not converted into authority for this sidewalk.

## Law After Decision

A government-secured sidewalk that substitutes for and continues an existing public street route under an enforceable passage obligation retains traditional-public-forum protection despite private title. This rule applies to that defined public route; it does not make commercial premises generally governmental. Existing content and neutral-management standards govern restrictions on the passage.

## Separate Writings

Justice Scalia dissents, joined by Justice Thomas. They would reverse and remand because governmental responsibility for this speech exclusion has not been established. Their framework requires attribution before forum classification: Lebron's concrete structural predicate and the limits emphasized in Brentwood do not allow public benefit, traffic regulation or access covenants alone to substitute for governmental control. The agreement expressly preserves present private rights and requires dedication if the present walkway is later removed, altered or abandoned. In their view, that distinction shows that the State secured passage and safety while withholding a present public speech entitlement. The fact that the walkway replaces a former public route makes the contrary argument substantial, but does not supply control over the disputed exclusion. They do not independently classify the forum after rejecting attribution. Their proposed remand would permit ordinary disposition under that attribution rule, rather than direct arrests, award damages or resolve a taking.

## Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit judgment remains in force. Lawful neutral regulation may address actual obstruction and safety under the governing public-forum standards. The decision concerns this replacement boulevard route and extends no access order to the casino interior or unrelated private property.

## Source Notes

[Venetian Casino Resort, L.L.C. v. Local Joint Executive Board, 257 F.3d 937](https://openjurist.org/257/f3d/937/venetian-casino-resort-llc-delaware-limited-liability-company-v-local-joint-executive-board-of-las-v), including the January 8, 1999 agreement and dissent. The agreement preserves private title and ordinary rights within constitutional limits while requiring continued connected public passage; the Court does not treat it as an express present conveyance of title. The County's enforcement position was neutral concerning forum classification.

---

<a id="ashcroft-v-free-speech-coalition-merits-2002-04-16"></a>

# Ashcroft v. Free Speech Coalition, No. 00-795 — 2002-04-16

[Authoritative Record](../records/ashcroft_v_free_speech_coalition_merits_2002-04-16.md)

## Event

Ashcroft v. Free Speech Coalition, No. 00-795; 535 U.S. 234. Argued October 30, 2001; decided April 16, 2002. On certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, 198 F.3d 1083, in a pre-enforcement facial challenge. The questions are whether §§2256(8)(B) and (D), covering apparent minority and presentation as depicting minors, substantially burden protected expression and whether faithful constructions or the enacted defenses save them. The Ninth Circuit reversed summary judgment for the Government, invalidated the two definitions, and treated the remaining categories as severable. No conviction involving an identified abused child is under review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Ninth Circuit judgment is affirmed on substantial-overbreadth grounds.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices |
|---|---|---|---|
| Section 2256(8)(B), youthful-looking actual adults | Affirm invalidation, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia |
| Section 2256(8)(B), wholly virtual realistic images | Affirm invalidation, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia |
| Section 2256(8)(D), presentation-based definition | Affirm invalidation, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia |

The Ninth Circuit judgment invalidating the challenged definitions is affirmed. Relief against enforcement through §§2256(8)(B) and (D) stands; the separate actual-child and obscenity prohibitions and the unchallenged identifiable-minor provision retain their independent operation. Substantial overbreadth supplies the ground without a separate Fifth Amendment vagueness judgment.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Affirms all three components | Parts I and II and all operative statutory qualifications |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | None | Affirms all three components | Present enforcement showing insufficient; preserves different narrowly drawn future question |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | O'Connor | Scalia in Part II only | Affirms youthful-adult (B) and (D); would sustain realistic wholly virtual (B) | Part I: separate invalidity grounds; Part II: virtual-image sustaining construction |
| Dissent | Scalia | None | Would reverse all three invalidations | Narrow conduct, apparent-age and knowing-pandering constructions |

## Holdings

**I. Apparent minority does not replace exploitation or obscenity.** A criminal definition substantially reaching nonobscene depictions produced without exploiting an actual child cannot be sustained merely because the depicted person appears to be a minor. Section 2256(8)(B)'s restriction fails the narrow tailoring required for this content-based burden on protected expression, including its wholly virtual realistic and actual-adult applications.

**Authority:** Parts I and II of Justice Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer. Those six directly support both rules and the statutory qualifications below.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Ferber's exclusion rests on injuries to actual children in production and circulation; visual resemblance creates neither injury by itself. Miller's independent obscenity elements remain necessary to that category. Reno and Playboy require the Government to justify this protected-speech burden, while Broadrick requires substantial protected reach relative to legitimate applications. The asserted grooming, demand and proof difficulties are serious interests, but they do not establish the fit required for this prohibition. A lawful image's possible misuse cannot itself supply production abuse, and difficulty proving an actual child does not permit suppression of substantial protected substitutes. A realistic-image construction does not cure that defect or supply the absent serious-value protection. Actual exploitation, obscenity and independently unlawful solicitation retain their own predicates.

**II. Presentation does not transform a protected work into contraband.** Section 2256(8)(D) is independently substantially overbroad because promotional presentation can make a nonobscene, nonexploitative work prohibited material in connected distribution and possession offenses, including for persons unconnected with the presentation. A court may not save this definition by rewriting it as a separately defined intentional unlawful-offer offense.

**Authority:** The same six directly join Part II and this independent invalidity ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Ginzburg makes pandering evidence relevant within an obscenity inquiry; it does not automatically remove protection from the underlying nonobscene work. The enacted definition is broader than the Government's proposed knowing-promoter construction, and ordinary scienter does not confine all connected liability to the original promoter. Reno permits genuine severance but not invention of absent offense limits. A differently enacted unlawful-offer offense lies outside the question, as does the validity of the identifiable-actual-minor definition in (C).

**Operative statutory qualifications.** A minor is under eighteen. Sexually explicit conduct covers actual or simulated genital-genital, oral-genital, anal-genital or oral-anal intercourse, whether between persons of the same or opposite sex, bestiality, masturbation, sadistic or masochistic abuse and lascivious exhibition of the genitals or pubic area; mere nudity does not itself establish the last category. Section 2252A(c) applies only to (a)(1)–(4), requires actual adult performers at production, and requires that the defendant did not advertise, promote, present, describe or distribute the work as depicting minors. It excludes wholly generated images and ordinary possession under (a)(5). Section 2252A(d), separately, concerns possession of fewer than three images: prompt, good-faith reasonable destruction steps or reporting the images to law enforcement and affording access to each image, without retaining any image or copy or permitting anyone other than law enforcement access to one. It requires reasonable destruction steps, not successful destruction, and provides no right to retain protected expression. The unchallenged (C) definition concerns a recognizable actual person who was a minor at the relevant creation, adaptation or modification or whose image as a minor was used; proof of the person's actual identity is unnecessary. These provisions do not cure the challenged protected reach.

## Precedent Treatment

- **New York v. Ferber, 458 U.S. 747, and Osborne v. Ohio, 495 U.S. 103:** Their actual-child injury predicates are preserved; they are not extended to an invented image or an adult performance solely because it resembles a child.
- **Miller v. California, 413 U.S. 15:** Obscenity remains an independent category with its own elements, not a substitute apparent-age rule.
- **Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union:** Its protected-adult-expression and substantial-overbreadth reasoning applies; its distinction between genuine severance and judicial rewriting defeats the proposed new offense limits.
- **United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc.:** Its content-based scrutiny and governmental justification burden govern the fit of the challenged criminal restrictions.
- **United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.:** Knowledge of depiction characteristics remains distinct from constitutional authority to suppress those characteristics; this case changes no surviving factual-knowledge requirement.
- **Broadrick v. Oklahoma, 413 U.S. 601:** Substantial protected reach, rather than a conceivable unusual application, supports facial relief here.
- **Ginzburg v. United States, 383 U.S. 463:** Pandering may inform an obscenity inquiry; it does not independently make a protected nonobscene work contraband for all possessors.

## Law After Decision

The two challenged definitions cannot support enforcement against the substantial protected expression they cover. Apparent minority without exploitation or obscenity does not suffice, and promotional characterization does not automatically transform a protected work into prohibited material. Independent actual-child, obscenity and unlawful-conduct rules retain their predicates. The identifiable-actual-minor category and a differently enacted unlawful-offer offense are outside this decision.

## Separate Writings

**Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** Thomas agrees with both invalidations but does not join the Court's principal reasoning. Enforcement of actual-child prohibitions is the Government's strongest concern. The present predictive showing does not establish a technological proof obstacle sufficient for this breadth, but practical impossibility of distinguishing fabricated from actual-child images could present a different constitutional question. An appropriate affirmative defense or another narrowly drawn restriction might matter on that demonstrated premise. He neither requires a particular future defense nor approves shifting the actual-child element through a current presumption. His agreement in judgment does not endorse a categorical foreclosure of every enforcement-based restriction affecting protected expression.

**O'Connor, concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part; Scalia joins Part II of her writing only.** In Part I she agrees with invalidation of the youthful-adult application: adult performers' protected expression remains exposed because the limited adult defense does not reach ordinary possession. She separately agrees that (D)'s presentation category lacks an adequately justified compelling fit, particularly when describing adult performers as minors removes the defense. In Part II she would preserve (B) for wholly virtual images virtually indistinguishable from actual-child images. The narrow construction excludes cartoons, sculptures and plainly unreal depictions. Congress may respond to supported enforcement predictions before actual proof failure becomes universal; in her view the child-protection interest and limited application satisfy the constitutional inquiry. Broadrick and application-severability principles permit preserving that application without extending the adult defense to possession. Her vagueness conclusion is confined to the realistic-image construction. Scalia joins only this virtual-image sustaining ground, not her adult or presentation invalidations.

**Scalia, dissenting.** Scalia would sustain both definitions under narrowing constructions. The statutory conduct requirement reaches actual or realistically simulated hardcore sexual acts, not merely a film's subject matter, and apparent minority means realistic images virtually indistinguishable from depictions of actual children. He adopts O'Connor's narrow virtual-image enforcement rationale but does not accept her adult-application invalidation. For (D), he reads offense scienter together with the definition to confine liability to knowing pandering by the responsible actor, excluding innocent downstream possession. Ginzburg supports the relevance of deliberate presentation to this confined transaction in his view. The Court regards that reading as alteration of a definition used in connected offenses and finds the remaining protected reach substantial. Scalia does not treat all nonobscene adult expression as unprotected or enlarge §2252A(c)'s defense.

## Procedure After Action

The judgment protecting the challengers against enforcement of §§2256(8)(B) and (D) remains in force. This merits review is concluded subject to the ordinary mandate; the surviving independent statutory categories remain enforceable on their own terms.

## Source Notes

[Free Speech Coalition v. Reno, 198 F.3d 1083](https://openjurist.org/198/f3d/1083); 18 U.S.C. §§2256(2), (8)(B)–(D), 2252A(c)–(d). [535 U.S. 234](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-535/pdf/USREPORTS-535-234.pdf), for the statutory text, facial posture and chronology. The identifiable-minor provision was not challenged; this judgment does not decide its constitutionality.

---

<a id="united-states-v-craft-merits-2002-04-17"></a>

# United States v. Craft, No. 00-1831 — 2002-04-17

[Authoritative Record](../records/united_states_v_craft_merits_2002-04-17.md)

## Event

United States v. Craft, No. 00-1831; 535 U.S. 274. Argued January 14, 2002; decided April 17, 2002. On certiorari to the Sixth Circuit, 233 F.3d 358, in the second appeal of an escrow dispute. The question is whether the taxpayer husband's pretransfer Michigan entireties rights constitute property or rights to property under 26 U.S.C. §6321. After assessment and lien notice, the spouses conveyed the estate to the wife; a later sale placed half the net proceeds in escrow by agreement. The circuit adhered to its first appeal under circuit precedent and law-of-the-case constraints, while rejecting the wife's cross-appeal. Attachment, valuation, priority and enforcement remain distinct; the alternative fraudulent-transfer theory is outside the reviewed question.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The reviewed no-attachment ruling is reversed, and the case is remanded.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Stevens, Scalia and Thomas.

The federal lien reaches the husband's qualifying pretransfer rights. The lower court must determine their value and the lawful consequence for the escrow, preserving the wife's independent rights and applicable priority and enforcement protections. The agreed escrow percentage is not an adjudicated valuation; the Court prescribes no automatic half recovery.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Reverses and remands | Rights-specific attachment and all remedial limits |
| Dissent | Thomas | Stevens, Scalia | Would affirm no attachment | Statutory ownership and inseparable-estate distinction |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm no attachment | Additional nondebtor-spouse institutional concern |

## Holdings

**The actual entireties rights are federal rights to property.** After demand and nonpayment, §6321 reaches the taxpayer's existing Michigan entireties rights considered together—present use, exclusion, enjoyment of income, survivorship and participation in joint disposition—even though the whole estate cannot be conveyed or partitioned unilaterally and is shielded from ordinary separate creditors. The lien reaches those taxpayer rights and creates no ownership of the wife's independent rights; their value and lawful collection consequences require separate determination.

**Authority:** Justice Breyer's Opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Those six directly adopt the attachment rule and all remedial limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Drye assigns definition of the rights to state law and their federal lien classification to federal law. Michigan's actual beneficial incidents remain valuable legal advantages despite consensual transfer restrictions. The contrary argument correctly begins with property belonging to the taxpayer, but incorrectly makes unilateral withdrawal or monetization indispensable without statutory text. National Bank of Commerce rejects the proposition that local creditor limitations alone settle federal reach. Bess separates the particular reachable economic right from other interests in the arrangement. No single incident, bare future expectancy or unified state label controls every case. Rodgers concerns enforcement and nondebtor protections, not automatic collection of a half share; Romani preserves specific priority rules. Attachment precedes the conveyance to the wife, while the escrow agreement's half share supplies no adjudicated valuation.

Remand must determine value and lawful treatment of the escrow while preserving the wife's independent interests, applicable priorities and enforcement protections. The excluded fraudulent-transfer theory remains outside this review; the Court does not reopen the unrelated cross-appeal merely by reversing the attachment premise. The former equal-share calculation and later $6,693 enhancement remedy are not adopted as the attachment value.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Drye v. United States:** Its state-definition/federal-classification method governs the actual present rights; its unilateral inheritance power is an application, not an exclusive condition silently added to §6321.
- **United States v. National Bank of Commerce, 472 U.S. 713:** Applied to distinguish state creditor limitations from federal lien classification.
- **United States v. Bess, 357 U.S. 51:** Applied to identify the particular reachable economic interest without transferring other interests in the arrangement.
- **United States v. Rodgers, 461 U.S. 677:** Enforcement and nondebtor protections retain their separate statutory requirements; attachment supplies neither an automatic sale nor a half-value allocation.
- **United States v. Estate of Romani:** Its specific priority protections remain applicable on their own predicates after attachment.

## Law After Decision

Michigan entireties restrictions do not defeat federal attachment to the taxpayer's existing beneficial rights considered together. The lien follows those actual rights, while their valuation, competing priorities, enforcement authority and the nondebtor spouse's independent interests retain separate legal work. The rule does not turn a bare future expectancy into property or assume identical marital-property law in every State.

## Separate Writings

**Thomas, dissenting, joined by Stevens and Scalia.** Section 6321 reaches property belonging to the taxpayer; it cannot create a distinct individual estate from inseparable marital ownership. Michigan gives the husband neither unilateral withdrawal nor a power to sell, partition or redirect the estate. Drye and National Bank of Commerce involved those actual powers over value, and therefore do not settle this case. Joint enjoyment and a veto on conveyance are meaningful benefits, but remain incidents of the marital entity's ownership rather than separately owned assets. The longstanding collection treatment supports this statutory caution; it does not replace the inquiry. The dissent does not make a creditor-exemption label conclusive or declare all restricted, nontransferable interests unreachable. It would affirm the attachment ruling without deciding a different tenancy, completed severance or separate income right.

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by Thomas.** Entireties ownership also gives institutional security to the nondebtor spouse, including a spouse whose domestic contribution yields no separate income. Removing that protection through a new federal property classification counsels particular statutory caution. This additional ground neither finds that Mrs. Craft held that domestic role nor creates a general marital-assets exemption. Stevens joins the common statutory dissent, but not this additional writing.

## Procedure After Action

Further proceedings determine valuation and lawful treatment of the escrow under the rights-specific attachment rule. The excluded transfer theory and the unrelated cross-appeal remain outside this review; the Court does not adopt either former lower-court remedial calculation as the attachment value.

## Source Notes

[Craft v. United States, 233 F.3d 358](https://openjurist.org/233/f3d/358), and 140 F.3d 638; 26 U.S.C. §6321; Mich. Comp. Laws §§557.71, 552.102. [535 U.S. 274](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-535/pdf/USREPORTS-535-274.pdf), for the chronology and Michigan incidents. The sale escrow preserved the dispute; its half-share arrangement did not establish the value of the taxpayer's rights.

---

<a id="a-and-m-records-v-napster-merits-2002-04-23"></a>

# A&M Records, Inc. v. Napster, Inc.; review of 239 F.3d 1004 — 2002-04-23

[Authoritative Record](../records/a_and_m_records_v_napster_merits_2002-04-23.md)

## Event

A&M Records, Inc. v. Napster, Inc. Merits decision, April 23, 2002, on full merits submission. Review of the Ninth Circuit judgment, 239 F.3d 1004, entered February 12, 2001, concerning the preliminary injunction entered July 26, 2000 and explained and slightly modified August 10, 2000. The Ninth Circuit affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. The questions concern the supported direct-infringement and secondary-liability predicates, sampling and space-shifting defenses, the Audio Home Recording Act and §512, the remaining owner-conduct and procedural objections, and the scope of preliminary relief. Napster's searchable directory facilitates music-file exchange; notices and account/index controls are established, but its servers do not possess users' MP3 contents. The review concerns the original restraint and its required correction, not a later replacement order.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Ninth Circuit's mixed preliminary disposition is affirmed, and the case is remanded for a conforming injunction and further proceedings.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Court sustains the supported preliminary infringement and secondary-liability predicates and the rejection of the asserted defenses at this record's scope. It affirms the requirement to replace the overbroad injunction with a fitted order and remands for that work. The original July 26, 2000 restraint remains stayed until modification conforming to this judgment. Owners must supply usable identification of works and infringing files; service responses must remain within actual capabilities and applicable statutory limits. These findings remain preliminary. The $5 million security is sustained.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Affirms the mixed preliminary disposition and remands | Parts I–VII in full; every stated qualification |

## Holdings

### I. The preliminary fair-use defenses fail under the integrated factors

**Holding and operative rule:** On this supported preliminary record, unrestricted exchange of complete protected creative recordings with unrelated users is unlikely to be fair use under §107. Nontransformative whole-work copying and supported substitution for existing and developing markets outweigh the claimed sampling or relocation purposes; artist-authorized and otherwise lawful files remain lawful.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by all eight other Justices. All nine directly support each of Parts I–VII, including the independent contributory and vicarious grounds and the qualifications below.

**Controlling explanation:** Campbell requires purpose, nature, amount and market effect together, without a dispositive commerciality presumption. The findings include displaced college sales and injury to a developing licensed-download market; this is not merely loss of fees for every unlicensed use. The contrary survey was weighed, not excluded. A plan to buy music later does not erase the present redistribution. Sony's private home time shifting does not establish immunity for public exchanges, and the claimed relocation extends beyond one's private personal devices. The result holds even assuming the owners bear the preliminary burden of likely defeating fair use; the allocation of that burden across all preliminary cases is unnecessary to resolve. The prima facie direct-infringement showing was unchallenged on this appeal; findings remain preliminary.

### II. Substantial lawful capability does not immunize specific ongoing assistance

**Holding and operative rule:** A service can incur contributory liability by knowingly providing material assistance to identified infringement, notwithstanding substantial noninfringing uses of its technology. Sony bars imputing the needed knowledge from capability alone; it does not immunize continuing materially assisting operation after specific knowledge.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion adopts this rule and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** The notices identified more than 12,000 infringing files, continued availability followed asserted account terminations, and the search and index functions materially assisted access. Those circumstances support likely knowledge and contribution. General knowledge that copyrighted music circulates does not establish unauthorized copying; lawful capability remains material and present infringement percentages are not an exclusive test. The rule requires no server access to MP3 contents the system does not possess.

### III. Financial benefit and actual supervision independently support preliminary vicarious liability

**Holding and operative rule:** Vicarious liability separately requires a direct financial benefit from infringement and the right and practical ability to supervise it. Attraction of infringing recordings to the user base can satisfy the benefit element without direct transaction fees, while account exclusion and index control support supervision over the service's actual assistance; both elements are supported here at the preliminary stage.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion adopts this rule and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** This is an independently sufficient preliminary ground, not a substitute for contributory knowledge. The architecture both supports limited control and restricts the remedy. A theoretical ability to close the entire service does not create a general duty to inspect inaccessible user files or guarantee prevention of unknown copying.

### IV. The Audio Home Recording Act is confined to its defined coverage

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1008 does not immunize this general-purpose computer service's open redistribution merely because users characterize copying as noncommercial. Its consumer-recording protection depends on the defined devices, media and recording acts.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion adopts this rule and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Under §1001(5), a digital musical recording is a material object containing, in digital form, only fixed sounds and related, relatively minor material, statements or instructions, from which the sounds and material can be perceived, reproduced or communicated directly or with a device. Entirely spoken-word recordings, including speech accompanied by merely incidental sounds, and objects containing computer programs are excluded; instructions constituting the fixed sounds and incidental material or bringing about their perception, reproduction or communication remain permitted. A digital audio recording device must be of a type commonly distributed to individuals for their use, whether separate or part of another machine, have a digital recording function designed or marketed primarily to make private digital audio copied recordings, and be capable of doing so. Professional model products and dictation, answering or other audio equipment designed and marketed primarily to fix nonmusical sounds are excluded.
