- **Trans World Airlines, Inc. v. Hardison, 432 U.S. 63:** Supplies a limited analogy about seniority expectations. Its Title VII protection of seniority and more-than-de-minimis hardship threshold do not become the ADA's rule.
- **Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc. and Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., June 22, 1999; Toyota Motor Manufacturing, Kentucky, Inc. v. Williams, January 8, 2002:** Their actual-disability rules remain separate from qualification and accommodation; the seniority decision neither changes those rules nor adjudicates the disputed coverage-preservation matter.
- **EEOC v. Waffle House, Inc., January 15, 2002:** Its independent public-enforcement and remedy rules remain intact; permission to pursue statutory relief is distinct from establishing actual entitlement.
- **Olmstead v. L.C., June 22, 1999, and PGA TOUR, Inc. v. Martin, May 29, 2001:** Their respective Title II resource and Title III fundamental-alteration rules retain their own predicates. Neither replaces Title I's reasonable-accommodation and undue-hardship inquiries.

## Law After Decision

Under original ADA Title I, established seniority ordinarily makes a conflicting reassignment unreasonable, even under an employer-created system. The employee can establish special circumstances making an exception reasonable; that opportunity is substantive and nonexclusive. If reasonableness is established, the employer still bears the distinct significant-difficulty-or-expense defense. Neutrality alone does not immunize an employment policy, effectiveness alone does not establish reasonableness, and seniority is neither merely a hardship factor nor an absolute exemption. The rule takes effect April 29, 2002. Actual application and the remaining claim conditions proceed under these settled rules.

## Separate Writings

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the Court's opinion in full and emphasizes that the special-circumstances showing must be a real and practical opportunity. Evidence that the employer retains power to change the system, that exceptions are already frequent, or that one more exception is unlikely to matter can carry the employee's showing in the particular case. Those avenues must receive substantive consideration rather than being treated as a formality. His full join preserves the Court's requirement to explain the actual effect on seniority expectations; retained power alone is not an automatic entitlement, and the employer's separate hardship defense remains.

**Justice Stevens, concurring.** He joins the full seniority presumption and remand. Seniority bears substantially on reasonableness, not only on hardship, but the employer's categorical position still fails. The existing interactive-process ground remains outside this seniority decision. Material application questions include why the mailroom position became open for bidding, whether the request is best understood as reassignment or continuation of the existing placement, and its actual effect on other employees. Barnett must answer the pertinent questions to establish special circumstances; the concurrence treats them as record-dependent inquiries, not a new mandatory checklist or findings in his favor.

**Justice O'Connor, concurring.** She joins the Court's opinion in full while explaining her preferred legal-entitlement rule. A position is unavailable when another employee occupies it or has an enforceable entitlement to it; an expectation under a noncontractual seniority policy alone supplies no legal possessor. On that approach, this policy does not defeat Barnett's initial reasonable-accommodation showing, and US Airways would have to establish actual hardship. She objects that a particularized special-circumstances inquiry can blur the distinction between general reasonableness and the employer's particular hardship burden. She nevertheless adopts the Court's statutory rule because a common rule is necessary and the practical exception will often produce the same result as her preferred approach. Disclaimers, reserved authority and actual exceptions can weaken expectations; her concurrence does not find that every noncontractual system does so. Her full join includes the employee's meaningful opportunity and the separate employer defense.

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by Justice Thomas.** They would reverse the seniority ruling and return for a disposition rejecting the demanded priority. Reading sections 12112(a) and (b)(5)(A) together, they require reasonable removal of workplace obstacles that disability prevents an employee from overcoming. A standing requirement or inaccessible workstation can be such an obstacle even though expressed neutrally. A bona fide seniority allocation unrelated to disability is different: an exception grants priority over coworkers rather than removing the disability-created barrier. Reassignment still requires the employer to seek and offer a suitable vacant job within the lawful allocation instead of simply dismissing a worker unable to perform the former job. Their Martin and Olmstead positions support that distinction at their respective statutory scopes, without supplying a substitute Title I rule. They reject the Court's special-circumstances exception as both beyond the statutory duty and uncertain even for established systems. Their ground is not immunity for every neutral policy, and their proposed disposition preserves independent unreviewed claims.

**Justice Souter, dissenting, joined by Justice Ginsburg.** They would affirm the rejection of employer summary judgment because Barnett has met the initial reasonableness showing, leaving US Airways to prove actual hardship. The ADA omits the express seniority protections found in Title VII and the ADEA; the committee reports likewise treat seniority as a consideration rather than a special exemption. Hardison's different statute and protected collective agreement do not justify a presumption for this employer's unilateral policy. They give reasonableness independent work through feasibility, suitability and the actual change requested. Barnett seeks to retain a job he performs, displaces no incumbent, and confronts a policy the employer disclaims as contractual and reserves power to change. Those circumstances satisfy the initial showing without establishing ultimate liability or eliminating evidence of operational disruption. They preserve the distinct federal force of enforceable labor contracts recognized in Lincoln Mills and Charles Dowd Box; no such agreement governs this request. The employer's proof of actual significant difficulty or expense, including concrete noncash effects, remains necessary under their view.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Ninth Circuit for further consideration of the seniority-accommodation claim under the ordinary seniority presumption, Barnett's special-circumstances opportunity and US Airways' separate undue-hardship defense. Ordinary summary-judgment rules govern the parties' showings. The independent cargo-accommodation and interactive-process rulings remain at their existing unreviewed scope; retaliation summary judgment remains affirmed.

## Source Notes

42 U.S.C. §§12111(8)–(10), 12112(a), (b)(5)(A); Fed. R. Civ. P. 56; Barnett v. U.S. Air, Inc., 228 F.3d 1105 (9th Cir. 2000) (en banc); US Airways, Inc. v. Barnett, No. 00-1250, 535 U.S. 391; argument transcript, December 4, 2001, p. 30. The handbook disclaims contractual status and reserves amendment; that reservation alone does not establish actual amendment frequency or the effect of an additional exception. Barnett seeks further adjudication, not summary judgment awarding the position.

---

<a id="ashcroft-v-aclu-merits-2002-05-13"></a>

# Ashcroft v. American Civil Liberties Union; No. 00-1293; 535 U.S. 564. — 2002-05-13

[Authoritative Record](../records/ashcroft_v_aclu_merits_2002-05-13.md)

## Event

Ashcroft v. American Civil Liberties Union, No. 00-1293, 535 U.S. 564. Argued November 28, 2001; decided May 13, 2002. October Term 2001. On writ of certiorari to the Third Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), reviewing 217 F.3d 162. The Third Circuit affirmed the February 1, 1999 preliminary injunction against the Child Online Protection Act on the ground that its community-standards feature independently made the statute facially invalid. The question is whether that feature alone supports the appellate disposition; other grounds for the preliminary injunction were not adjudicated by that rationale.

COPA, 47 U.S.C. §231, reaches knowing commercial World Wide Web communications, made with knowledge of the material's character in interstate or foreign commerce, available to any person under seventeen and containing material harmful to minors. Commercial purpose requires a regular business course with a profit objective and knowing posting or solicitation of the material; actual profit and a sole or principal business are unnecessary. This statutory category does not make protected expression commercial advertising. Harmful material includes obscenity or material satisfying all three requirements: taken as a whole and with respect to minors, prurient appeal or pandering judged by the average person applying contemporary community standards; patently offensive depictions, descriptions or representations of sexual acts or contacts, actual or simulated and including normal or perverted acts, or lewd displays of genitals or post-pubescent female breasts, with respect to minors; and, taken as a whole, lack of serious literary, artistic, political or scientific value for minors. The community-standards phrase appears expressly in the prurience clause.

Subsection (a) permits a fine up to $50,000, imprisonment up to six months, or both; intentional violations carry an additional fine up to $50,000 per violation, and the separate civil penalty is also up to $50,000 per violation. For the latter two, each day is a separate violation. Subsection (b)(1)–(3) excludes a telecommunications carrier to the extent it provides telecommunications service, an Internet-access-service provider in that capacity, and an information-location service in that capacity. Subsection (b)(4) separately protects intermediaries transmitting, storing, retrieving, hosting, formatting or translating another's communication without selecting or altering its content; deletion consistent with subsection (c) or §230 does not defeat that paragraph's protection. Subsection (c)(1) supplies a good-faith access-restriction defense through a credit card, debit account, adult access code or adult personal identification number, an age-verifying digital certificate, or other reasonable measures feasible with available technology. Subsection (c)(2) separately protects good-faith implementation or restriction activity against a cause of action only when that activity violates no law carrying a criminal or civil penalty. Subsection (d) separately requires protection against unauthorized access to collected information and prior written or electronic consent for disclosure, from the adult concerned or the parent or guardian of a person under seventeen. Disclosure is permitted when necessary to communicate, to conduct a legitimate related business activity, or under an authorizing court order.

The preliminary findings include speakers' inability to exclude Web visitors geographically and verification burdens affecting expense, anonymity and adult access. Those findings remain part of the inquiry; the Court does not substitute assumed future technology for the existing record.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court vacates the reviewed appellate judgment and remands; the existing preliminary injunction remains in force.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 8–1.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Stevens, who would affirm the preliminary-injunction judgment on the community-standards ground.

The Third Circuit's judgment is vacated insofar as it treats the community-standards feature as sufficient by itself. The case returns for consideration of the statute's full operation and the remaining First Amendment grounds. The existing preliminary injunction remains in force during that consideration. This disposition authorizes no prosecution merely because the categorical appellate rationale fails.

## Opinion Topology

KENNEDY, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and O'CONNOR, SCALIA, SOUTER, THOMAS and GINSBURG, JJ., joined, and in which BREYER, J., joined as to Parts I and III. O'CONNOR, J., filed a concurring opinion. THOMAS, J., filed a concurring opinion, in which SCALIA, J., joined. SOUTER, J., filed a concurring opinion, in which KENNEDY and GINSBURG, JJ., joined. BREYER, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and in the judgment. STEVENS, J., filed a dissenting opinion.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg throughout; Breyer in Parts I and III only | Vacates and remands with the injunction intact | Part I: statutory background and posture; Part II: confined no-automatic-invalidity holding and explanation; Part III: disposition and continuing injunction |
| Concurrence | O'Connor | No other Justice | Joins the Court's opinion and judgment | Insufficient present showing of substantial overbreadth from variation alone; affirmative support for a permissible national standard; future supported challenges remain possible |
| Concurrence | Thomas | Scalia | Joins the Court's opinion and judgment | Broader defense of community standards with the conjunctive prurience and objective-value restrictions; no complete validation of COPA |
| Concurrence | Souter | Kennedy, Ginsburg | Joins the Court's opinion and judgment | Integrated community-standards challenge remains available after statutory scope is determined; actual adult-access burdens remain material |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Joins Parts I and III and the judgment; does not join Part II | National adult-community construction is necessary to his reasoning; no approval of varying local standards |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Geographic adult-speech burden suffices for the preliminary injunction; rejects substitution of a national standard |

Part II has seven direct joins. Breyer's separate construction supplies an additional judgment vote, not an eighth join in that rationale. No broader separate theory commands a majority.

## Holdings

### Community standards alone do not establish COPA's facial invalidity

**Holding and operative rule.** COPA's use of contemporary community standards does not, standing alone, establish facial invalidity: constitutional review must assess the complete offense, its limiting elements and defenses, and its actual burden on protected expression together. This rejection of automatic invalidity supplies neither a presumption that COPA survives nor a holding that varying local standards are constitutional in every application to the Web.

**Authority.** Part II of Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg, supplies seven direct votes for this confined rule and explanation. Part III's vacatur, remand and preservation of the injunction also receive Breyer's join. The Court adopts no national-community construction and uses no narrowest-grounds aggregation.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the First Amendment inquiry concerns the enacted restriction as a whole. Reno protects adult Internet expression against substantially overbroad criminal restrictions; it does not turn every reference to community standards in a different law into an independent constitutional defect. COPA contains commercial-coverage, whole-material, prurience, specified-content and serious-value limits that must do their actual legal work. Miller supplies the obscenity framework, and Pope separates serious value from local acceptance. Their relevance does not establish that material lacking serious value for minors is unprotected for adults.

The challengers' strongest objection rests on the existing finding that Web speakers cannot exclude visitors geographically: a restrictive community may consequently burden expression nationwide. The Court preserves that fact and the verification burdens on cost, anonymity and access. But geographic variation alone does not determine the protected reach of all the statutory requirements operating together. Nor does the presence of limiting elements establish that their combined operation satisfies the Constitution. Substantial overbreadth requires examination of protected expression in relation to the statute's legitimate reach, not isolation of one phrase.

Reno and Playboy continue to require justification of the actual protected-adult-speech burden, including consideration of concrete, plausibly effective, less restrictive means. The statutory commercial-purpose label does not reduce literary or other protected expression to advertising. Each defense, provider exclusion and privacy provision retains its own conditions. Free Speech Coalition distinguishes ordinary construction from judicial addition of a new offense limitation. The Court therefore neither mandates a national standard nor finally approves local standards. The complete constitutional challenge and unresolved tailoring grounds return because the reviewed appellate rationale did not decide them. The preliminary injunction remains while that work proceeds.

**Precedent treatment.** Reno's adult-access holding remains controlling and its categorical extension to any community-standards phrase is rejected; Miller and Pope supply distinct obscenity and objective-value principles; Playboy preserves the governmental justification burden; Free Speech Coalition preserves substantial-overbreadth review and the boundary against judicial rewriting. No precedent is overruled.

## Precedent Treatment

- Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844: applied to protected adult access and practical scrutiny; distinguished as to COPA's different enacted elements. Its invalidation of the CDA's broader restrictions remains effective.
- Miller v. California, 413 U.S. 15: applied for the conjunctive obscenity framework; its acceptance of community standards does not itself resolve COPA's effect on protected adult speech.
- Pope v. Illinois, 481 U.S. 497: applied to keep serious value independent of local community acceptance; the objective-value inquiry is not a complete answer to adult access.
- Hamling v. United States, 418 U.S. 87, and Sable Communications of California, Inc. v. FCC, 492 U.S. 115: their treatment of community standards remains available, but does not automatically resolve a restriction on nonobscene adult expression through the Web. Their addressed-distribution settings do not erase the geographic findings here.
- United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc., 529 U.S. 803: applied for the Government's ultimate justification burden and examination of concrete, plausibly effective, less speech-restrictive alternatives; no perfect alternative or prescribed legislative-record format is required.
- Ashcroft v. Free Speech Coalition, 535 U.S. 234, and Broadrick v. Oklahoma, 413 U.S. 601: applied to substantial protected reach relative to legitimate scope; the former also preserves the difference between ordinary interpretation and adding absent offense limitations.

## Law After Decision

Lower courts must evaluate COPA's actual statutory reach and adult-speech burden as a whole. The presence of community standards alone cannot sustain the reviewed categorical facial rationale, and the presence of other elements alone cannot establish ultimate validity. Neither a national standard nor categorical approval of local standards becomes controlling law. The existing adult-speech, substantial-overbreadth and justification rules remain operative. Full statutory validity, the factual adequacy of verification and the comparative effectiveness of alternative protections remain for the returned proceeding because the appellate rationale did not adjudicate them.

## Separate Writings

**O'Connor, concurring.** She joins the confined holding because the present showing does not identify sufficient otherwise protected material made unlawful solely through community variation once prurience and serious value are considered. Pope's objective-value inquiry and Sable's distinction between an adult ban and confined protection matter, but the Web's inability to select recipients prevents treating conventional mail and telephone distribution as conclusive. She affirmatively regards a national standard as constitutionally permissible and necessary for reasonable Internet obscenity regulation. Her judgment neither forecloses an adequately supported facial challenge nor denies later as-applied relief.

**Thomas, concurring, joined by Scalia.** The prurience and objective serious-value requirements materially narrow the relevant category compared with the CDA. Miller, Pope, Hamling and Sable support rejecting community variation as a sufficient objection to this statutory category; the existing showing does not establish substantial variation in the covered material. This broader explanation would reject the community-standards objection more fully than the Court's common ground. It does not adopt a national-standard amendment, resolve COPA's other constitutional defects, or treat statutory commercial purpose as permission to suppress protected adult literature. They join continued interim protection while the other injunction grounds are considered.

**Souter, concurring, joined by Kennedy and Ginsburg.** The extent of protected expression cannot be measured until the statute's reach is determined. Commercial coverage, the relevant whole work or communication, the different ages within the under-seventeen category and venue consequences affect the relationship between legitimate regulation and protected expression. Reno, Playboy and Free Speech Coalition require attention to actual adult access, not an assumption that value for minors cures the adult burden. These Justices expressly preserve the community-standards challenge as part of the integrated inquiry: after construction, geographic variation may still prove decisive. Their join does not approve local standards as constitutionally harmless, depend on hypothetical geographic controls, or adopt a national standard.

**Breyer, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He joins the statutory background and disposition but reads community standards to mean the national adult community. The ordinary phrase in context, its legislative explanation and avoidance of serious constitutional difficulty support that construction in his view. A local standard would give restrictive communities practical control over protected speech distributed nationally. Variation among juries applying one national standard is different from variation among the governing substantive standards themselves. This construction is necessary to his agreement, not a merely optional possibility. It does not resolve the other COPA objections, and he agrees that the injunction should continue.

**Stevens, dissenting.** He would affirm the preliminary injunction on the community-standards ground. His objections in Smith and Sable, together with Reno's adult-access rule, apply to the established inability of national Web speakers to avoid restrictive local audiences. Serious value for minors does not define all expression adults may constitutionally receive. Narrowing the commercial category or the whole-work unit may reduce absolute coverage without curing substantial overbreadth relative to legitimate applications. Verification burdens do not erase that injury. He rejects insertion of a national standard into language with an established community-based meaning. His position preserves the legitimate interest in protecting children and ordinary restrictions on unprotected material; it does not require invalidating every future Internet safeguard.

## Procedure After Action

The Third Circuit must reconsider the injunction dispute under the whole-statute inquiry while the existing preliminary injunction remains effective. Its work includes commercial coverage, content breadth, the whole-work unit, differences among minors' ages, the placement and operation of defenses, criminal consequences, chat rooms, discussion groups, links and foreign access insofar as properly presented, verification expense and anonymity, and concrete less restrictive alternatives. The existing findings remain available; the decision imposes no new technological experiment or automatic reopening of proof. Ordinary mandate procedure governs the return.

## Source Notes

47 U.S.C. §231(a)–(e); 28 U.S.C. §1254(1); ACLU v. Reno, 217 F.3d 162 (3d Cir. 2000), including notes 16, 19 and 21–24; Smith v. United States, 431 U.S. 291; Sable, 492 U.S. 115; Pope, 481 U.S. 497. The findings concerning geographic access and verification burdens arise from the preliminary-injunction record. The judgment under review did not adjudicate all grounds for that injunction.

---

<a id="city-of-los-angeles-v-alameda-books-merits-2002-05-13"></a>

# City of Los Angeles v. Alameda Books, Inc.; No. 00-799. — 2002-05-13

[Authoritative Record](../records/city_of_los_angeles_v_alameda_books_merits_2002-05-13.md)

## Event

City of Los Angeles v. Alameda Books, Inc., No. 00-799. Argued December 4, 2001. Decided May 13, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, reviewing its judgment, 222 F.3d 719, filed July 27, 2000 and amended August 28, 2000, sustaining summary judgment, a declaration and an injunction for the businesses. The question is whether the City's concentration evidence can support the asserted noncommunicative harm underlying its combined-use prohibition. The district court's preserved expressive-targeting ground remains distinct; the court of appeals assumed neutrality and held the evidence insufficient.

Los Angeles's 1977 study linked concentrations of adult establishments to neighborhood crime but counted bookstore/arcade combinations as single establishments. Each plaintiff's retail and viewing operations occupies under 3,000 square feet, with one entrance and one supervising employee. The premises are physically undivided and the revenues are distinguished only internally. Neither plaintiff violates the separate one-thousand-foot adult-business or five-hundred-foot school, park or religious-institution distance rule. Those facts do not by themselves establish the actual effects of patron concentration.

Section 12.70(C) separately prohibits establishing or maintaining multiple adult businesses in the same building, structure or portion, and increasing an adult business's floor area in premises containing another adult business. Each defined adult-business category counts separately even when operated in one commercial enterprise. The combined-use amendment allowed combinations to continue until March 10, 1985, except a business already required to discontinue under the earlier transfer provision. That earlier provision required discontinuance within five years of a transfer allowed during the original ordinance's two-year transfer period if the business remained within the prohibited adult-business or school, park or religious-institution distance. After March 10, 1985, pre-September 1, 1978 uses could continue; if none qualified, one use could continue, with the earliest established taking priority in a dispute.

Continuation to March 10, 1987 could be granted for a written lease made before March 10, 1982 and extending beyond March 10, 1985, or undue financial hardship from pre-March 10, 1982 investment in real property, improvements or stock. An uncorrected violation of the governing zoning chapter after notice barred continuation. The verified application required prescribed supporting information, without a fee; a decision was due within seventy-five days unless the applicant and administrator agreed to an extension. A hearing could be set if necessary. The determination could not take effect until fifteen days after a copy of the written determination was mailed to the applicant; a timely appeal stayed proceedings on the challenged action pending determination. These terms establish no qualification of either plaintiff.

The separate exception for a site within five hundred feet of an A or R zone requires that no compliant site be reasonably available elsewhere. A reasonably available site must satisfy applicable zoning, be available for use, purchase or rent, have adequate street access, lighting and sidewalks, and be at least five hundred feet from uses that are or may become obnoxious or offensive through odor, dust, smoke, noise, gas, fumes, cinders, refuse or water-carried waste. Massage parlors and sexual-encounter establishments are excluded. The applicant must identify its current or proposed site and supply supporting data and the fee. A hearing and decision are required within sixty days; approval is required when the conditions are met, and denial requires findings identifying the deficiency. An appeal must be decided within thirty days after expiration of the appeal period. This is a residential-zone exception, not an exception to every combined-use prohibition.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Ninth Circuit judgment is vacated, and the case is remanded.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

**Opposing:** Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The City's initial concentration inference defeats the stated evidence-only summary-judgment ground. The preserved expressive-targeting, advancement, fit and alternative-expression questions return for adjudication under the scrutiny warranted by the ordinance's actual operation and justification. The ordinance receives no final validation. Existing injunctive relief is subject to prompt lawful reconsideration on remand; this merits vacatur alone does not authorize immediate enforcement.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Vacates and remands | Parts I–II in full: initial inference, preserved classification and fit inquiries, nonvalidating remedy |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Joins the Court's opinion and judgment | Additional necessary speech-preservation condition: harmful conditions must be dispersed while the quantity and accessibility of protected expression remain substantially intact |
| Dissent, Part I | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Fuller content-correlation, classification and nonsuppression account; Breyer does not join |
| Dissent, Part II | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Present evidence fails to connect the compelled division to reduced noncommunicative harms, even assuming genuine secondary-effects scrutiny |

## Holdings

#### I. The concentration study supports the limited inference necessary to defeat this evidentiary summary-judgment ground

**Holding and operative rule.** The City's neighborhood-concentration study and reasonable inferences from it suffice to defeat the businesses' summary-judgment theory that the combined-use amendment necessarily lacks a relevant evidentiary basis because the study counted a bookstore and arcade combination as one establishment. A city may rely on reasonably relevant concentration evidence without separately studying every internal building configuration, but that initial sufficiency does not establish neutrality, final efficacy, adequate fit or constitutional validity.

**Authority.** Part I of Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court is joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. All five directly support this confined evidentiary proposition. Kennedy's additional speech-preservation ground is separately stated; it does not create agreement with every broader secondary-effects formulation.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the studied concentration of adult operations can support an inference about their concentration within one building. Renton permits reliance on evidence reasonably believed relevant to the noncommunicative harm addressed; it does not demand architectural replication. Turner preserves substantial respect for supported prediction while requiring independent judicial examination of the inference.

The study counted combined bookstore and arcade premises as one unit, and the plaintiffs occupy small, physically undivided premises. Those facts support a serious challenge to the City's theory, but they do not make the inference legally unavailable. Concentrating distinct operations can concentrate activity relevant to the identified neighborhood harm even without a separate exterior wall. The City need not prove that concentration is crime's exclusive cause or conclusively establish the ordinance's success at this initial stage.

The court of appeals did not announce an absolute new-study requirement; it rejected this inference. The Court disagrees with that conclusion on the asserted evidentiary ground. The businesses may still show that the evidence fails to support the City's actual rationale or supply contrary evidence and practical reasoning. A material challenge requires renewed support from the City, which retains the ultimate justification burden. No presumption that expression causes harm follows. The separate question whether compelled division alleviates the demonstrated harm without impermissibly burdening protected expression remains governed by the scrutiny applicable to the ordinance's actual justification and operation.

**Precedent treatment.** Renton's reasonably relevant evidence principle applies to the initial concentration inference; it does not establish the ordinance's final validity. Turner's deferential but independent review of predictive judgments remains intact.

#### II. The preserved targeting and fit grounds require adjudication before the ordinance can be upheld

**Holding and operative rule.** On remand, the courts must examine the preserved expressive-targeting ground and the ordinance's actual advancement, fit and access to protected expression under the scrutiny its operation and justification warrant. Under City of Erie, a regulation whose actual justification and operation principally target communicative adult entertainment cannot receive incidental-burden treatment merely because the City describes its concern as secondary effects; the City's initial evidentiary showing does not discharge its ultimate constitutional burden.

**Authority.** The same five Justices join Part II of the Court's opinion, adopting this remand rule and its nonvalidating consequence. It applies the directly controlling Erie targeting boundary without adopting Erie's separate nonmajority strict-scrutiny account or its independent evidence analysis as a whole.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that evidentiary relevance and constitutional classification do different work. The court of appeals assumed neutrality and therefore did not resolve the district court's preserved targeting ground. Erie requires attention to actual operation and justification. O'Brien supplies the requirement of a governmental interest unrelated to suppressing expression for incidental-burden treatment; Renton preserves genuine land-use regulation of noncommunicative harms, subject to adequate alternative avenues. If the actual classification requires stricter review, the Government must satisfy that review rather than borrow a more permissive label.

The businesses may defend the existing decree on the preserved ground without seeking an enlarged decree, as El Paso Natural Gas permits. Recognizing an initial concentration inference therefore cannot itself remove that ground or determine final validity. The causal support, the burden of separating these operations and alternative expressive opportunities require adjudication on the actual record. Ordinary evidence and reasoning can test the City's predictive account; no mandatory new local study or new hearing is created. The City must justify its response under the applicable standard, and the courts must promptly reconsider the existing injunction consistently with that inquiry.

**Precedent treatment.** City of Erie supplies the actual-targeting boundary; O'Brien supplies its nonsuppression predicate; El Paso Natural Gas preserves the alternative ground for the existing decree. None makes the initial study a complete constitutional defense.

## Precedent Treatment

- Renton v. Playtime Theatres: applied to reasonably relevant evidence and genuine land-use treatment of noncommunicative harms; its label cannot decide this preserved targeting issue.
- Turner Broadcasting System v. FCC: applied for respect for supported predictive judgment combined with independent examination; no conclusory municipal assertion becomes conclusive.
- City of Erie v. Pap's A.M.: its direct targeting boundary controls classification on remand. Its fuller nonmajority strict-scrutiny and evidence rationales are not imported as law.
- United States v. O'Brien: its nonsuppression requirement remains a necessary premise for incidental-burden review.
- Ward v. Rock Against Racism: preserves advancement and proportionate burden for genuine neutral regulation without requiring the least restrictive means.
- United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group: preserves the Government's ultimate burden when actual content-selected suppression warrants strict scrutiny; the present initial-inference holding does not predetermine that classification.
- El Paso Natural Gas v. Neztsosie: applied to permit defense of the existing decree on its preserved alternative ground without enlargement.

## Law After Decision

The law permits this initial concentration inference while requiring the preserved classification, advancement, fit and expressive-access inquiries. The ordinance receives no final constitutional validation. Kennedy's more specific speech-preservation formulation remains his separate condition, and the dissent's present-record insufficiency conclusion does not become the Court's law.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, concurring and joining the Court's opinion in full.** He accepts the initial inference only within a genuine dispersal theory. The ordinance must reduce harmful conditions while leaving the quantity and accessibility of protected expression substantially intact. Reducing speech or its audience, and thereby reducing associated harms, is not a permissible secondary-effects mechanism. Renton's land-use category and Turner's actual-operation inquiry permit a supported concentration inference, but the economic premise that the ordinance disperses rather than suppresses remains open to meaningful challenge. His illustrations supply no finding about patron numbers, successful relocation or business survival. This distinct necessary condition is not an additional majority holding.

**Souter, dissenting, Part II, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** Even assuming the genuine secondary-effects framework, the study fails to connect isolated combined operations with the harm caused by the concentration of separate establishments. Counting the bookstore and arcade as separate legal categories does not show more patrons or criminal opportunity in these small undivided premises. Compelling their separation may double operating costs and curtail expression without dispersing any demonstrated harm. That is an objection to the City's inferential bridge, not a finding that closure will occur. Renton permits relevant outside studies and ordinary experience, but the support must address the change being regulated; no perfect study is demanded. The dissent would preserve the businesses' declaration and injunction on this sufficient evidentiary ground.

**Souter, dissenting, Part I, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg only.** Content-correlated regulation requires independent attention to actual effects and evidentiary safeguards against disguised suppression. O'Brien's nonsuppression premise and Erie's targeting rule prevent the selected adult-content category from receiving automatic deference. The separate evidence ground suffices for affirmance without a final classification ruling. Breyer does not join this broader account.

## Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit judgment is vacated, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the preserved classification, advancement, fit and expressive-access grounds consistently with the initial-inference holding. The courts must promptly reconsider existing injunctive relief under the governing standards. The ordinance is not finally upheld, and the vacatur alone supplies no automatic enforcement permission.

## Source Notes

The [petition appendix](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0428) supplies the Ninth Circuit opinion, Appendix A, and the enacted municipal provisions, including the separate combined-use, amortization and residential-zone provisions. The [official report, 535 U.S. 425](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-535/pdf/USREPORTS-535-425.pdf), supplies the authenticated argument date and judicial-record recitations. The ordinance's existence, its category definitions and its exceptions do not establish that the plaintiffs qualify for an exception or that compelled division will cause a particular closure, relocation or crime effect. The City's concentration inference is an initial evidentiary conclusion; ultimate constitutional justification and the preserved alternative ground remain for adjudication.

---

<a id="henderson-v-norris-merits-2002-05-13"></a>

# Henderson v. Norris; reviewed Eighth Circuit No. 00-2203, 258 F.3d 706 (2001); no Supreme Court docket supplied. — 2002-05-13

[Authoritative Record](../records/henderson_v_norris_merits_2002-05-13.md)

## Event

Henderson v. Norris. Merits decision, May 13, 2002, on full merits submission. Review of the Eighth Circuit judgment, 258 F.3d 706, entered July 9, 2001 in No. 00-2203, reversing the denial of federal habeas relief and directing a conditional sentencing writ. The State seeks restoration of the habeas denial. The question is whether life imprisonment with no ordinary parole unless executive commutation first reduces the sentence to years is grossly disproportionate for this first conviction of delivering 0.238 grams of cocaine base, under the governing pre-AEDPA and nonretroactivity rules.

The completed twenty-dollar sale occurred April 23, 1993. Trial began August 29, 1994, and the Arkansas Supreme Court affirmed on November 13, 1995. Henderson's federal petition preceded April 24, 1996. The record includes evidence of two additional rocks of cocaine and an effort to prevent a prosecution witness from testifying by providing transportation and expenses to travel to another State. The Eighth Circuit reports that the sentencing jury was not instructed to use the additional acts in fixing punishment and declined to presume that witness interference supplied the sentence's basis. That is the appellate court's recitation of the charge, not a separate finding that the conduct did not occur.

The sentence is Arkansas life imprisonment, with ordinary parole available only if discretionary executive commutation first reduces it to years. It is distinct from the separately named capital-murder and treason sentence that precludes parole even after commutation. Clemency provides no assured release date. The comparison record concerns Arkansas maximum authorized penalties, later advisory guidelines subject to the statutory minimum and aggravating departures, and reported penalties elsewhere; authorization of a maximum is distinct from actual imposition.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at full merits submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Eighth Circuit judgment is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

The Eighth Circuit judgment granting a conditional sentence-only writ is affirmed. Arkansas has ninety days after the Eighth Circuit issues its mandate implementing this judgment to resentence Henderson lawfully, subject to ordinary mandate administration. The conviction remains intact. The lawful replacement sentence is for the State to determine under the constitutional rule stated here; the affirmed conditional remedy gives it the stated opportunity before unconditional relief becomes operative.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Common review framework | Pre-AEDPA temporal rule, independent constitutional legal review, and distinct Teague inquiry; no merits-relief agreement inferred |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts II–III | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Affirms conditional sentence-only relief | Dictated application of finality-era law, sentence-specific gross disproportionality and conditional remedy |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | O'Connor throughout; Thomas only in the assumed-framework application | Joins Part I; would reverse on the sentence claim | Kennedy and O'Connor endorse the narrow proportionality framework; Thomas assumes it for the application rejecting relief |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | No other Justice | Joins Part I; would reverse on the sentence claim | Distinct rejection of a prison-duration proportionality right, not a join in the other dissent's application |

## Holdings

#### I. Pre-AEDPA independent legal review and Teague remain distinct inquiries

**Holding and operative rule.** Henderson's noncapital federal habeas application, filed before April 24, 1996, is governed by pre-AEDPA law rather than amended section 2254(d); supported factual findings retain the treatment required by the former law, while the federal constitutional legal question receives independent review. Teague does not substitute general deference to every reasonable state legal interpretation for that review, but relief still requires a result dictated by precedent when the conviction became final or an actually applicable retroactivity exception.

**Authority.** Part I of Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court is joined by all eight other Justices. All nine adopt this temporal and review distinction; only the five identified in Parts II–III adopt the separate old-rule and sentence-relief conclusions.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the filing date governs the statutory question. Lindh excludes the ordinary chapter 153 amendments at issue from this pre-enactment noncapital application. The later standard for unreasonable state adjudications therefore cannot decide this claim. Miller v. Fenton supplies the distinction between respect for supported facts and independent evaluation of the constitutional legal question.

The State invokes Teague to demand acceptance of reasonable state interpretations even in this earlier proceeding. Nonretroactivity performs different work. It bars a newly announced constitutional rule from supplying collateral relief unless an applicable exception permits it; it is not a general replacement standard for legal review. The published record does not establish a separately developed defense identifying some other specific new rule or an independent waiver of such a defense. The Court must nevertheless determine whether the relief it grants follows from finality-era law. It makes that determination below rather than treating rejection of the State's proposed deference rule as abolition of Teague.

**Precedent treatment.** Lindh governs the specified amendments' temporal application; its provision-specific limits remain. Miller v. Fenton supports independent constitutional legal evaluation. Teague retains its distinct finality-era inquiry.

#### II. Existing law dictates the sentence-specific result

**Holding and operative rule.** The finding of gross disproportionality for this first conviction, this exceptionally small completed sale and life imprisonment without ordinary parole applies the law already established by Rummel, Solem and Harmelin before Henderson's conviction became final. Relief therefore depends on no new categorical first-offender exemption, drug-weight ceiling or retroactivity exception.

**Authority.** Part II of Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer. All five support the complete old-rule conclusion and its stated boundaries. The four Justices who reject relief do not join it merely because they accept independent pre-AEDPA review.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that identifying an old general principle is necessary but not sufficient: the result must follow from the earlier law at the relevant level of specificity. Henderson's direct-review affirmance occurred in 1995, after Rummel, Solem and Harmelin. No disputed exact subsequent finality date affects the availability of those precedents.

Solem already distinguished discretionary executive commutation from ordinary parole and required assessment of the actual offense and culpability. Rummel's repeated convictions and meaningful parole eligibility are absent. Harmelin preserves a narrow proportionality inquiry and sustained punishment for a quantity capable of extensive distribution; it does not make every retail sale equivalent to that large-scale offense. Together those settled distinctions require attention to the extraordinary combination present here. The comparisons corroborate the exceptional mismatch; they create no new sentencing schedule.

The contrary position would give legislative latitude conclusive force despite the surviving threshold. The Court gives that latitude substantial weight, but it cannot erase the actual offense or convert discretionary grace into parole. Neither disagreement about the application nor the absence of a prior case involving precisely 0.238 grams makes the result new. Graham's distinction between applying a dictated protection and extending it supports the inquiry; its capital-mitigation holding supplies no imprisonment rule. The Court changes no threshold and grants relief only for the existing constitutional violation.

**Precedent treatment.** Teague and Graham preserve the distinction between dictated application and new constitutional extension; Rummel, Solem and Harmelin supply the finality-era substantive boundaries actually applied.

#### III. Permanent imprisonment is grossly disproportionate on this offense and offender record

**Holding and operative rule.** A life sentence with no ordinary parole eligibility is grossly disproportionate for Henderson's first conviction of delivering 0.238 grams of cocaine base on the established record. The rule reaches materially equivalent first-offender conduct and punishment; it neither equates sale with possession nor invalidates every life sentence for a drug offense or fixes a maximum lawful replacement term.

**Authority.** The same five Justices join Part III of the Court's opinion, directly supporting this sentence-specific application and the conditional sentence-only remedy. The Court does not derive a new imprisonment formula from the distinct law of monetary forfeitures.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the severity of the punishment must be compared with this offense and offender. Solem supplies that individualized comparison and the distinction between parole and executive grace. Harmelin's narrow proportionality principle requires substantial legislative latitude and comparative inquiry only after the exceptional initial inference arises. Permanent imprisonment for this first conviction and tiny completed sale crosses that threshold.

The record concerns a completed twenty-dollar delivery, not innocent possession. It includes evidence of two additional rocks and interference with a witness. Even considering the additional rocks, it supplies no Harmelin-scale distribution predicate. The Eighth Circuit reports that the sentencing jury was not instructed to use the additional acts in selecting punishment; the Court does not transform that appellate recitation into an independently examined trial charge or presume that witness interference supplied the sentence's basis.

The comparisons confirm the mismatch at their supported scope. Arkansas authorizes life as a maximum for first-degree murder, rape and kidnapping. Later advisory guidelines do not govern Henderson; their three-and-one-half-year suggestion remains subject to a ten-year statutory minimum and permits aggravating departures, including for witness interference. Other-State evidence distinguishes authorizing life from actually imposing it and retains the uncertainty about Idaho parole. The reported federal ranges—ten to sixteen months, or fifteen to twenty-one with an obstruction enhancement—corroborate disparity without dictating a constitutional term. No complete nationwide survey or numerical ratio decides the case.

The State rightly emphasizes distribution's grave social harms. Those harms justify severe punishment, but they cannot substitute a hypothetical violent enterprise, recidivism or the entire drug trade for this individual's proved conduct. Arkansas retains the task of selecting a lawful replacement sentence in a lawful sentencing proceeding.

**Precedent treatment.** Solem's meaningful proportionality and parole distinction are applied; Rummel is distinguished on recidivism and parole; Harmelin's narrow standard is applied to a materially different offense magnitude. None is overruled.

## Precedent Treatment

- Lindh v. Murphy: applied to exclude amended section 2254(d) from this pre-April 24, 1996 noncapital petition; no exemption from every AEDPA provision is announced.
- Miller v. Fenton: applied to separate independent constitutional legal review from the former treatment of supported facts.
- Teague v. Lane: applied as a distinct nonretroactivity limit, not a general deference standard; relief rests on an old-rule application, without reliance on an exception.
- Rummel v. Estelle: distinguished because its recidivism and meaningful parole predicates are absent.
- Solem v. Helm: applied for actual-offense proportionality, corroborative comparison and the difference between ordinary parole and discretionary grace.
- Harmelin v. Michigan: its surviving narrow gross-disproportionality principle governs; its wholesale-quantity application does not decide every completed drug sale.
- Graham v. Collins: applied only for the distinction between dictated application and extension of old law; its capital-mitigation result does not govern imprisonment duration.
- United States v. Bajakajian: remains confined to its fines framework and actual-offense application; its monetary proportionality rule is not converted into a prison-term formula.

## Law After Decision

Under the existing narrow proportionality rule, permanent imprisonment for this first conviction and materially equivalent tiny-sale conduct is unconstitutional. The conviction remains intact. The law establishes neither a general drug-weight schedule nor the replacement term. Pre-AEDPA independent legal review and Teague's separate finality requirement retain their distinct operation.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Parts II–III, joined by O'Connor throughout the separate writing and by Thomas in the assumed-framework application only.** The dissent would restore denial of the sentencing claim. The tiny quantity, first conviction and lack of ordinary parole make the sentence severe, but the completed distribution offense and supported record do not meet the extraordinarily demanding threshold under Harmelin. Legislative judgments can account for distribution's preventive and social harms without measuring sentence length arithmetically by weight. The comparison evidence does not convert the exceptional severity into a constitutional prohibition. The dissent acknowledges the real difference from Harmelin's quantity and treats clemency as discretionary grace; it assumes neither a large enterprise nor prior convictions. Thomas's agreement assumes narrow proportionality for this disposition and does not adopt its constitutional foundation. Merits denial makes a separately unestablished new-rule bar unnecessary to these positions.

**Scalia, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Parts II–III.** Harmelin's distinct textual and historical account rejects prison-duration proportionality as an independent requirement of the Cruel and Unusual Punishments Clause. The actual sentence and comparisons may present a sentencing-policy objection, but they do not establish the asserted constitutional right on his ground. His acceptance of the Excessive Fines inquiry in Bajakajian supplies no prison-duration rule. He accepts the controlling temporal law and does not rely on amended section 2254(d) or a fictitious criminal history. His rationale is distinct from the other dissenters' conclusion that this sentence passes a narrow proportionality test.

## Procedure After Action

The Eighth Circuit judgment is affirmed. Its implementing mandate begins Arkansas's ninety-day period for lawful resentencing under the conditional sentence-only writ, subject to ordinary mandate administration. The conviction remains in force. The Court specifies no maximum replacement term; lawful state sentencing must comply with the constitutional boundary announced here.

## Source Notes

The [Eighth Circuit opinion, 258 F.3d 706](https://ecf.ca8.uscourts.gov/opndir/01/07/002203P.pdf), supplies the offense, additional evidence, attributed instruction account, pre-AEDPA filing, comparative record and conditional remedy. Henderson v. State, 322 Ark. 402, 910 S.W.2d 656 (1995), supplies the direct-review disposition. The exact subsequent finality date is not established, but Rummel, Solem and Harmelin all preceded the 1995 direct-review affirmance. The [official Harmelin report](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-501/pdf/USREPORTS-501-957.pdf) supplies its distinct rationales and quantity-specific application. The original trial charge is not independently reproduced. The other-State comparison retains the Idaho parole uncertainty and distinguishes authorized maxima from actual sentences; it is not a complete national statutory survey.

---

<a id="lapides-v-board-of-regents-merits-2002-05-13"></a>

# Lapides v. Board of Regents of University System of Georgia; No. 01-298; 535 U.S. 613. — 2002-05-13

[Authoritative Record](../records/lapides_v_board_of_regents_merits_2002-05-13.md)

## Event

Lapides v. Board of Regents of University System of Georgia, No. 01-298, 535 U.S. 613. Argued February 25, 2002; decided May 13, 2002. October Term 2001. On writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1), reviewing 251 F.3d 1372. The question is whether a State that voluntarily removes an action to federal court relinquishes its federal-forum immunity from state-law claims for which it already permits adjudication in its own courts.

A university professor sued the Board and individual officials in Georgia court, alleging that placement and dissemination of sexual-harassment allegations in his personnel files violated state tort law and his federal rights. Those assertions remain allegations. The Georgia Attorney General removed the action on the asserted federal-question basis and sought dismissal. The district court treated removal as waiver; the Eleventh Circuit rejected that conclusion because state law did not separately authorize the Attorney General to waive immunity.

Georgia's Tort Claims Act waives immunity only to the extent and in the manner the Act provides and only for actions in Georgia courts; it expressly retains immunity in federal courts. The Georgia Constitution requires legislative waiver and preserves federal constitutional immunity. The Attorney General's representation authority supports the State's actual removal, while separate authority to declare an immunity waiver is disputed. The State's §1983 damages-defendant status, the individual defendants' claims and defenses, original federal jurisdiction and supplemental jurisdiction remain distinct from the federal-forum immunity issue.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The judgment of the Eleventh Circuit is reversed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Eleventh Circuit's determination that Georgia retained its federal-forum immunity from these state-law claims despite authorized voluntary removal is reversed. The case returns for ordinary jurisdictional and claim-specific proceedings consistent with the waiver holding. The decision does not order that the action be sent to state court; the proper treatment of the remaining claims follows the original- and supplemental-jurisdiction rules stated below.

## Opinion Topology

THOMAS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SCALIA, KENNEDY, SOUTER, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Reverses the no-waiver judgment | Complete confined removal-waiver rule, limited Ford displacement, independent Will rule, jurisdictional distinctions and ordinary further proceedings |

All nine join the opinion in full. No separate writing is filed.

## Holdings

### I. Authorized voluntary removal relinquishes federal-forum immunity for claims the State permits in its own courts

**Holding and operative rule.** A State that authorizes voluntary removal of an action to federal court relinquishes its federal-forum immunity from state-law claims for which it has already waived immunity in its own courts, even though its law expressly reserves federal-forum immunity and does not separately authorize counsel to declare a waiver. That actual choice of federal adjudication has a uniform federal consequence; it does not enlarge substantive liability, surrender unrelated defenses or decide removal of claims for which the State retains immunity in its own courts.

**Authority.** Thomas's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls both holdings and all stated qualifications by nine direct joins.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a State cannot select a federal tribunal for claims it permits in its own courts and then defeat adjudication solely because the chosen tribunal is federal. Clark, Gunter and Gardner recognize the legal consequences of actual submission to federal adjudication. Removal is a voluntary invocation of that adjudication even when the State originally appeared as a defendant.

Georgia's contrary argument correctly distinguishes authority to remove from separate state-law authority to announce an immunity waiver. The conclusion does not follow. The former authority makes the removal the State's own act, and federal law supplies its forum consequence. Requiring a second internal authorization would permit incompatible positions about the very tribunal the State selected. The clarity inquiry concerns that deliberate litigation act; it does not depend on counsel's motive, a prediction of advantage or an abstract balancing of dignity against injury.

College Savings Bank and Florida Prepaid preserve this actual-consent route while rejecting consent inferred from ordinary regulated commerce. Georgia's state-court tort waiver alone supplies no federal consent. Its later authorized removal supplies the operative act. Schacht kept that removal-waiver question open; Raygor preserved an asserted defense during litigation in a forum the State did not select by removal. Neither requires the incompatible forum positions here.

Ford's authorization rationale cannot control insofar as it would preserve federal-forum immunity after this authorized voluntary invocation. Ford is overruled to that extent. The Court retains the Georgia Act's substantive scope and manner conditions and confines the rule to the state-law claims before it; claims immune in the State's own courts and an otherwise valid federal claim lie outside the question necessary to decide this controversy.

**Precedent treatment.** Clark, Gunter and Gardner are applied to authorized voluntary removal; College Savings Bank and Florida Prepaid retain their distinction between actual submission and constructive commercial consent; Schacht's reserved issue is resolved at this scope; Raygor is distinguished; Ford is overruled only insofar as its contrary authorization rationale applies to this invocation.

### II. Forum waiver leaves the cause of action and jurisdictional inquiries distinct

**Holding and operative rule.** Removal waiver does not make the State a person subject to the pleaded §1983 damages claim or cure a defective cause of action; Will's statutory rule applies independently. The district court must establish original jurisdiction separately and apply the mandatory remand rule if it is absent; when original jurisdiction existed but the federal claims are dismissed, §1367(c)(3) permits discretionary treatment of the remaining state claims rather than requiring either federal retention or return to state court.

**Authority.** The unanimous Court opinion identified above directly adopts these rules and their application to the returned proceeding.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that consent to a forum is not a new cause of action. Will excludes the State from the persons subject to the pleaded §1983 damages action. That defect remains despite removal and waiver. It does not collapse the separate questions whether the complaint supplied original federal jurisdiction when removed and whether the individual defendants face viable claims.

Schacht distinguishes a claim's immunity from the action's otherwise valid federal jurisdiction; Steel Co. likewise keeps jurisdiction separate from the merits of a statutory cause. Failure of a federal claim therefore does not automatically establish that original jurisdiction never existed. The district court must examine the actual jurisdictional anchors. Section 1447(c) governs a case lacking subject-matter jurisdiction. If jurisdiction originally existed and all federal claims are then dismissed, §1367(c)(3) allows the court to decline supplemental jurisdiction. The state claims remain a live controversy, and their lawful retention or remand follows that separate inquiry. The waiver holding neither compels their federal adjudication nor adjudicates tort liability or the individual defendants' defenses.

**Precedent treatment.** Will is applied as an independent statutory defendant rule. Schacht and Steel Co. preserve the distinction between jurisdiction, claim defenses and merits; no failure of the State-directed federal claim automatically determines the remaining action's forum.

## Precedent Treatment

- Clark v. Barnard, 108 U.S. 436: its voluntary-submission rule is applied to the State's deliberate selection of a federal tribunal.
- Gunter v. Atlantic Coast Line Railroad Co., 200 U.S. 273: its actual-litigation-submission principle supports the consequence of choosing federal adjudication.
- Gardner v. New Jersey, 329 U.S. 565: its invocation-of-federal-adjudication principle supports requiring submission within the invoked proceeding.
- College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board and Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank: their actual-consent distinction is applied; ordinary commercial activity remains insufficient to establish consent. Their separate constitutional-power holdings are unchanged.
- Wisconsin Department of Corrections v. Schacht, 524 U.S. 381: its claim-specific jurisdictional distinction is applied, and its open removal-waiver issue is resolved only for the state-permitted state-law claims described here.
- Raygor v. Regents of University of Minnesota, 534 U.S. 533: distinguished because retained immunity during discovery, mediation and scheduling did not involve voluntary removal. Its exclusion of a nonconsenting State's immune supplemental claims from §1367(d) remains effective.
- Ford Motor Co. v. Department of Treasury, 323 U.S. 459: overruled insofar as its state-authorization rationale would preserve federal-forum immunity following the authorized voluntary invocation decided here. Other questions about defensive participation and unrelated propositions are not displaced.
- Will v. Michigan Department of State Police, 491 U.S. 58: applied independently to the State-directed §1983 damages claim; forum waiver supplies no statutory person.
- Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment, 523 U.S. 83: applied to keep jurisdiction distinct from the failure of a statutory merits theory.

## Law After Decision

The open removal-waiver question is now resolved for state-law claims that a State allows against itself in its own courts: authorized voluntary removal relinquishes the federal-forum immunity objection despite an earlier express reservation. The substantive scope and manner of the State's waiver, valid causes of action and independent defenses continue to govern liability. Ford's inconsistent internal-authorization rationale cannot defeat this consequence. Removal of claims for which state-court immunity remains intact and an otherwise valid federal claim are outside this decision's confined question. Existing rules of statutory defendant status, original jurisdiction and discretionary supplemental jurisdiction remain separate and operative.

## Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns following reversal of the appellate immunity ruling. The district court must apply Will to the State-directed damages cause, determine the actual original-jurisdiction basis and address the individual defendants and remaining claims separately. Absence of original jurisdiction invokes the applicable mandatory remand command; dismissal of federal claims after jurisdiction attached calls for ordinary §1367(c)(3) discretion over supplemental claims. The Georgia tort conditions and independent defenses govern any further adjudication. Ordinary mandate procedure applies.

## Source Notes

Lapides v. Board of Regents, 251 F.3d 1372 (11th Cir. 2001), including note 2; 28 U.S.C. §§1254(1), 1331, 1367(a), (c)(3), 1441(a), 1447(c); 42 U.S.C. §1983; O.C.G.A. §§45-15-3(6), 50-21-23(b); Ga. Const. art. I, §II, para. IX(e), (f). The waiver issue concerns authorized removal and the state-permitted tort claims. The allegations in the complaint are not findings of liability.

---

<a id="verizon-communications-v-fcc-merits-2002-05-13"></a>

# Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC; Nos. 00-511, 00-555, 00-587, 00-590 and 00-602; 535 U.S. 467. — 2002-05-13

[Authoritative Record](../records/verizon_communications_v_fcc_merits_2002-05-13.md)

## Event

Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC, 535 U.S. 467; consolidated Nos. 00-511, 00-555, 00-587, 00-590 and 00-602. Argued October 10, 2001; decided May 13, 2002. October Term 2001. On writs of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit, reviewing 219 F.3d 744 under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1). After the earlier AT&T decision, the court of appeals sustained forward-looking pricing in principle but invalidated the efficient-network assumption in 47 C.F.R. §51.505(b)(1) and the additional combination rules in §51.315(c)–(f). Incumbent carriers challenge the costing authority and methodology; the Commission and competing carriers seek restoration of the invalidated rules.

The Telecommunications Act requires incumbent carriers to furnish authorized unbundled network elements on just, reasonable and nondiscriminatory rates and terms. Section 252(d)(1) requires rates based on the cost of providing the interconnection or element, determined without reference to a rate-of-return or other rate-based proceeding, and permits a reasonable profit. The pricing rule uses total element long-run incremental cost, or TELRIC, plus reasonable forward-looking common costs. Its efficient-network assumption retains existing wire-center locations while using currently available efficient technology and a lowest-cost configuration. This is review of general methodology and duties, without an adjudicated particular rate, complete carrier financial record or specific combination request.

Section 251(c)(3) requires access in a manner allowing requesting carriers to combine elements. Paragraphs (c) and (d) require requested necessary combining functions in their respective settings; the Commission's contemporaneous explanation addresses assistance for entrants lacking the facilities or network information needed to combine. That explanation and the paragraphs' distinct text are construed below. Existing-combination protection under paragraph (b), lawful element selection under §251(d)(2), state implementation and lawful compensation remain separate.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The consolidated judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part, and the cases are remanded with the challenged regulations restored at the stated scope.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded.** The consolidated dockets receive the following component dispositions; no separate docket outcome is implied.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Forward-looking pricing authority; claimed mandatory historical-cost recovery | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Retain the rejection of compulsory embedded-cost pricing |
| Efficient-network assumption in §51.505(b)(1) | Invalidation reversed, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Breyer | Restore the challenged methodology with its statutory and regulatory limits; Breyer would retain partial vacatur for agency reconsideration |
| Present facial confiscation objection and requested constitutional historical-cost construction | Rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | No facial constitutional invalidation or mandatory historical-cost construction; supported challenges to actual rates remain available |
| Additional combination rules, §51.315(c)–(f) | Invalidation reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Restore the rules under the necessary-assistance construction and distinct conditions and proof requirements below |

The cases return for proceedings implementing those rulings. Particular rates, engineering requests, compensation disputes and entitlement to an element require their own statutory application and supported facts. The judgment imposes no new element list or guaranteed reimbursement of past investment.

## Opinion Topology

SCALIA, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, KENNEDY, SOUTER, THOMAS and GINSBURG, JJ., joined, and in which BREYER, J., joined except as to Part II. BREYER, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg throughout; Breyer in Parts I, III and IV | Supports all component dispositions | I: forward-looking authority and no mandatory historical cost; II: actual efficient-network methodology; III: present facial constitutional objection; IV-A: necessary combination assistance; IV-B: distinct conditions, feasibility and proof |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | No other Justice | Agrees with Parts I, III and IV; would retain partial pricing vacatur for agency reconsideration | Objects only to Part II's acceptance of the efficient-network benchmark's explanation concerning replacement investment and facilities competition |

Parts I, III and IV have nine direct joins. Part II has eight. The common grounds are direct majority holdings, without aggregation of separate rationales.

## Holdings

### I. The statute permits forward-looking cost and does not require embedded-cost recovery

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 252(d)(1) permits the FCC to adopt a reasonable forward-looking economic measure of the cost of providing an interconnection or network element; it does not require reimbursement of the incumbent's embedded investment or every historical expenditure. Rates must remain just, reasonable, cost-based and nondiscriminatory, with cost determined without reference to a rate-of-return or other rate-based proceeding; reasonable profit is permitted rather than independently guaranteed.

**Authority.** Part I of Scalia's Opinion of the Court is joined by all eight other Justices. The nine-Justice coalition also directly joins Parts III and IV. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg join Scalia in Part II, giving that part eight votes; Breyer does not join Part II.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the enacted term cost does not select the incumbent's books as the sole lawful measure. AT&T establishes national implementing authority while preserving state rate determinations.
