Florida Prepaid permits consideration of a respondent's properly presented unchanged-judgment alternative; resolving those grounds only as necessary does not enlarge the grant to every telecommunications or immunity question.

Ex parte Young permits prospective restraint of connected officers' ongoing federal violations. The threshold examines the allegation and requested relief, not whether Verizon has proved its federal claim. This statutory scheme does not impose a restrictive substitute remedial sequence that the prospective action would evade. Seminole Tribe permits its officer route within enacted remedial limits; Coeur d'Alene preserves the properly limited route without generalized sovereign-interest balancing as a bar. Schacht keeps jurisdiction, defendants and immunity distinct.

Restraint of continued enforcement is prospective even when it changes private carriers' financial positions. Effective restraint may operate against the enforcing commissioners without the commission itself. That conclusion defeats categorical dismissal; it does not decide all other parties or prayers. A declaration affecting accrued private obligations and a direction concerning private-carrier refunds require their own analysis below. No refund entitlement is established. Edelman's boundary against retrospective State compensation and Pennhurst's boundary against federal commands enforcing state law remain.

Direct Commission amenability, regulatory-participation consent, sufficient authorization of direct-State suit and the private statutory cause need not be decided to preserve this officer claim. Florida Prepaid's Article I rule remains available on its own constitutional and unmistakable-authorization predicates; the Court finds none of those predicates here merely from participation. College Savings Bank does not equate continued ordinary commercial activity with consent or determine how this regulatory role should be classified, and Lapides's voluntary-removal circumstances are not present. The lower court must adjudicate federal compliance and tailor any permissible remedy rather than assume the compensation claim succeeds.

### IV. The judgments are vacated and the cases returned

**Controlling proposition:** The Fourth Circuit's judgments in both consolidated dockets are vacated and the cases remanded for adjudication under the jurisdictional and prospective-officer rules stated above. Remaining defendant, claim, preclusion and remedy questions must be addressed at their actual scope; the compensation merits are not resolved by this threshold decision.

**Authority:** Part IV of Thomas's opinion is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplying eight direct votes. Stone-Zsela joins Parts I–III but would reverse and remand rather than vacate.

**Controlling explanation:** The categorical barriers to federal adjudication cannot sustain dismissal, while distinct issues remain for the lower courts under the governing rules. Vacatur removes the judgments supporting that dismissal without resolving the remaining claims and prayers. The available prospective route is a right to adjudication on the stated conditions, not a present adjudication of federal unlawfulness or an award of intercarrier payments.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Bell v. Hood, 327 U.S. 678 (1946):** Applies its substantial-federal-claim distinction with both complete exceptions; eventual merits failure is separate from jurisdiction.
- **Ex parte Young, 209 U.S. 123 (1908):** Applies the route against connected officers for prospective restraint of an alleged ongoing federal violation.
- **Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, March 27, 1996:** Applies the officer-route holding within enacted remedial limits; it supplies no categorical exclusion of this action or Article I bar.
- **Idaho v. Coeur d'Alene Tribe of Idaho, June 23, 1997:** Preserves the established officer route and rejects generalized sovereign-interest balancing as an independent bar; the State-property setting does not transform these intercarrier consequences into State damages.
- **Wisconsin Department of Corrections v. Schacht, June 22, 1998:** Applies the separation between federal jurisdiction and a particular defendant's immunity.
- **Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, June 23, 1999:** Applies its unchanged-judgment alternative-ground rule; preserves its Article I enforcement rule without finding the predicates of direct-State suit here.
- **College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, June 23, 1999:** Preserves the rejection of consent inferred from continued ordinary commercial activity; classification of this regulatory role remains undecided, and no automatic waiver follows.
- **Lapides v. Board of Regents, May 13, 2002:** Its voluntary-removal rule does not establish consent through the regulatory participation presented here.
- **Edelman v. Jordan; Pennhurst State School and Hospital v. Halderman:** Preserve, respectively, the retrospective State-compensation and state-law-command boundaries.
- **AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, January 25, 1999; Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC, May 13, 2002:** Preserve the distinct federal/state allocation, methodology and combination holdings; they do not determine this review channel or Internet-bound reciprocal compensation.

## Law After Decision

Section 1331 remains available for the substantial federal enforcement challenge even on the assumption that §252(e)(6)'s determination-review sentence does not apply. Its separate failure-to-act exclusivity remains intact. Responsible commissioners may face prospective restraint of ongoing enforcement alleged to violate federal law, without treating private financial effects alone as State damages or the Commission's absence as an automatic bar. Full determination-review coverage, direct Commission amenability, consent and the private statutory cause remain undecided because this limited review does not require them. Federal compliance, compensation merits and the availability and scope of the actual requested remedies require ordinary adjudication below under the stated rules; establishing the forum does not decide those matters.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I–III and dissents from the form of the judgment. He would reverse and remand because §1331 and the prospective officer-relief route permit the claim against the responsible commissioners to proceed. He agrees that federal jurisdiction, statutory authority, consent and remedy do separate work; participation does not establish constructive consent, and intercarrier financial consequences do not make the requested future-enforcement restraint a State-damages action. His reversal would reinstate adjudication of the federal claim, leaving federal compliance and the precise injunction for decision below. He would not determine direct Commission waiver or the compensation merits. His disagreement concerns reversal rather than vacatur, not the controlling threshold rules or the requirement of further adjudication.

## Procedure After Action

The Fourth Circuit's judgments in both dockets are vacated and the cases remanded. The lower courts must permit adjudication consistent with the §1331 and prospective-officer holdings, determine federal compliance and address the actual remaining party, preclusion and remedial issues. Any injunction must follow that adjudication and observe the stated prospective and federal-law limits. The earlier Maryland state-court judgment is not vacated.

## Source Notes

28 U.S.C. §1331; 47 U.S.C. §§251, 252(e)(3)–(6), 253; Verizon Maryland Inc. v. Public Service Commission of Maryland, No. 00-1531, and United States v. Public Service Commission of Maryland, No. 00-1711; reviewed judgment 240 F.3d 279 (4th Cir. 2001). Review is limited to Question 2 and necessary alternative grounds for sustaining dismissal. The later administrative order, prior state judgment and the distinct requests for prospective restraint, declaration and private refunds retain their separate legal character.

---

<a id="Bell-v-Cone-Merits-2002-05-28"></a>

# Bell v. Cone; No. 01-400; 535 U.S. 685. — 2002-05-28

[Authoritative Record](../records/Bell_v_Cone_Merits_2002-05-28.md)

## Event

Bell v. Cone, No. 01-400, 535 U.S. 685. Merits decision, May 28, 2002. Argued March 25, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit.

The questions concern presumed prejudice from counsel's failure to present new mitigation witnesses and waiver of closing at capital sentencing, and the availability of federal habeas relief from the state ineffective-assistance adjudication. The Sixth Circuit affirmed denial of conviction relief but granted a sentence-only conditional writ requiring a new penalty proceeding within 180 days after remand. It left other sentencing grounds unaddressed. The federal application was filed July 1, 1997; the challenged state merits adjudication was entered September 19, 1986, followed by appellate affirmance on November 4, 1987 and denial of permission to appeal by the Tennessee Supreme Court on March 14, 1988.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at submission or argument and decision. Nine participate; the quorum is six and a majority is five.

## Public Action

The Court issues its signed merits decision.

## Judgment & Remedy

The Sixth Circuit's Cronic-based grant of sentence relief is reversed and the case remanded, 8–1. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

Relief is denied on the reviewed available-mitigation and closing-argument claim because the state performance determination was not an objectively unreasonable application of Strickland. The remaining, independently pending sentencing grounds return for ordinary adjudication, including the distinct investigation/development default questions to the extent legally available. The convictions are unaffected. Reversal removes the conditional sentence writ on this ground; it does not restore the district court's entire sentencing disposition or resolve the remaining appeal.

## Opinion Topology

BREYER, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and O'CONNOR, SCALIA, KENNEDY, SOUTER, THOMAS and GINSBURG, JJ., joined. STEVENS, J., joined Part I and filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; Stevens in Part I only | Reversal and bounded remand | Parts I–IV by the eight-Justice coalition; Stevens joins only the statutory and prejudice framework in Part I |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm Cronic relief; alternatively would return the ordinary tactical claim too | Rejects Parts II–IV's applications and final ordinary-claim disposition; preserves the common framework |


## Holdings

### I. The statutory collateral standard and ordinary prejudice requirement

**Controlling proposition:** The July 1, 1997 federal application is governed by amended §2254. Clearly established constitutional law is measured at the relevant state merits adjudication; a state application must be objectively unreasonable, not merely incorrect, and ordinary Strickland prejudice requires a reasonable probability of a different result without an additional fundamental-unfairness or unreliability requirement for lawful mitigation.

**Authority:** Part I of Breyer's opinion is joined by all nine Justices at this framework level. Stevens does not join the Court's Cronic application or its final rejection of the ordinary tactical claim.

**Controlling explanation:** Lindh makes the application date decisive for the amendment's applicability. Strickland and Cronic existed when the State adjudicated the claim; Williams supplies the Court's present construction of the statutory review standard. Its contrary-to and unreasonable-application inquiries remain distinct, and its standard is not satisfied or defeated merely by pointing to judicial disagreement. The district court's added Fretwell requirement cannot govern lawful mitigating evidence. That error does not itself establish entitlement to the writ. A separately sufficient, correctly assessed performance ground can defeat the confined claim without deciding actual prejudice. Later opinions cannot create an April 1982 professional duty or constitutional law unavailable at the state merits date.

**Precedent treatment:** Lindh v. Murphy, June 23, 1997, supplies the application-date distinction. Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362 (2000), supplies objective statutory review and rejection of an extra Fretwell hurdle for lawful mitigation. Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668 (1984), retains its separate performance and ordinary-prejudice requirements.

### II. These omissions within actual advocacy do not trigger Cronic's presumption

**Controlling proposition:** Counsel's failure to call additional mitigation witnesses and waiver of closing do not amount to a complete failure of adversarial testing in this sentencing proceeding, where counsel substantively presented mitigation already in evidence, sought mercy, obtained favorable cross-examination and resisted damaging prosecution proof. The challenged choices require Strickland analysis; the conclusion neither excuses abandonment nor eliminates other recognized circumstances in which Cronic presumes prejudice.

**Authority:** Parts II–IV of Breyer's opinion are joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, including the author in that eight-Justice coalition. Stevens dissents from these applications and the judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** Cronic addresses a complete loss of the relevant adversarial function, not every failure within representation. Counsel's opening directed the same jury to the guilt-stage experts and mitigating circumstances, invoked military service and remorse, and requested mercy. Cross-examination elicited the Bronze Star and honorable discharge, and objections excluded damaging prosecution material. These acts supplied adversarial sentencing assistance rather than physical presence alone. Cone argues that guilt-stage insanity evidence did not become a sufficient individualized case for life after conviction, and that the isolated acts left the decisive prosecution case unanswered. Those objections concern the quality and adequacy of the assistance actually given. They do not establish the complete failure on which this claimed presumption depends. No numerical tally of acts or universal rule that any activity defeats every Cronic category follows.

**Precedent treatment:** United States v. Cronic, 466 U.S. 648 (1984), is applied to the claimed complete-failure category; its other recognized grounds for presumed prejudice remain. Strickland governs the particular alleged errors within this represented proceeding.

### III. The confined tactical claim fails on a sufficient performance ground

**Controlling proposition:** On the reviewed available-mitigation and closing-argument record, the state court's rejection of deficient performance was not an objectively unreasonable application of Strickland. That sufficient ground defeats this confined claim without an actual-prejudice determination; it does not decide distinct allegations of failure to investigate or develop mitigation, their default treatment, or every remaining sentencing ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The ordinary claim was addressed below and is fairly included in review of the state rejection. Concrete reasons support its disposition: credited reliance on experts whose testimony remained before the jury, counsel's assessment of his mother's ineffective appearance, interviews of other potential witnesses, the reviewed record's lack of a showing that omitted testimony would help, and avoiding the more effective prosecutor's unanswered rebuttal. Neither a strategy label nor the district court's statement that strategy was unreviewable supplies the rule.

Cozzolino limited punishment proof to statutory aggravation or mitigation actually raised; rebuttal must explain or controvert a proposition advanced. Its troubled-childhood evidence did not make present criminal proclivities relevant. Later defense proof can make premature admission harmless only by supplying actual rebuttal relevance. Thus a general fear of all bad-acts questioning would be mistaken, but no rule immunized every possible character statement. The Court makes no new witness-specific admissibility finding.

Pritchett provided pretrial support for resentencing when one of two aggravators failed and the remaining weighing could not be assumed. It guaranteed no new hearing whenever any aggravator failed. Cone's 1984 direct appeal later distinguished the surviving aggravators and applied harmless error; that later ruling is not counsel's April 1982 knowledge. Counsel's testimony supplied multiple reasons for waiving closing, including existing mitigation and avoiding the particular rebuttal. The Court endorses no general practice of deliberately creating appellate error.

This independent state-performance assessment does not depend on the district court's erroneous additional prejudice requirement. The restriction of the reviewed record to available evidence makes preservation of separate development and default questions essential; broad praise of counsel's preparation does not decide them.

**Precedent treatment:** Williams and Strickland are applied to an independently sufficient, context-specific performance ground. Cozzolino v. State, 584 S.W.2d 765 (Tenn. 1979), and State v. Pritchett, 621 S.W.2d 127 (Tenn. 1981), retain their actual relevance and aggravator limits; Cone v. State, 665 S.W.2d 87 (Tenn. 1984), remains separately dated.

### IV. The remaining sentence matters require their own adjudication

**Controlling proposition:** The Cronic-based sentence writ is reversed and the confined ordinary tactical claim rejected; the Court of Appeals must adjudicate the remaining properly pending sentence claims and distinct investigation/default issues under their own legal requirements. The convictions remain undisturbed, and no blanket reinstatement of the district court's entire sentencing judgment follows.

**Controlling explanation:** The Sixth Circuit expressly left other sentencing challenges unaddressed. The separate investigation allegations raise disputed questions about the adequacy and independence of the second-petition bar and asserted cause from unavailable investigative funding. Cone's remand argument presents those issues but establishes neither cause nor invalidity of the state ground. Lee concerns a different state procedural application and does not resolve Tennessee's. Ordinary further adjudication determines any necessary proceedings; the mandate does not itself direct an evidentiary hearing or funding.

**Precedent treatment:** Lee v. Kemna, January 22, 2002, retains its exceptional state-ground holding and is not extended into a determination of these separate default issues.

## Precedent Treatment

Cronic's complete-failure doctrine remains distinct from Strickland's particular-error inquiry. Williams's objective unreasonable-application rule and ordinary lawful-mitigation prejudice rule govern; the district court's additional Fretwell burden is rejected. Lindh determines which statutory review regime applies. Cozzolino and Pritchett retain their specific Tennessee limits, with the later Cone ruling kept separate from counsel's trial-date knowledge. Lee supplies no automatic resolution of the distinct state-default challenges. Roe v. Flores-Ortega, Mickens v. Taylor, Alabama v. Shelton and United States v. Cotton retain their separate functional, conflict, counsel-at-adjudication and direct-review holdings; they create no new rule applicable retroactively to this state merits adjudication.

## Law After Decision

Actual sentencing advocacy prevents treating these particular omissions as complete adversarial abandonment. Ordinary tactical choices remain reviewable under Strickland and the applicable §2254 standard; a strategy label is not conclusive. The independently reasonable state-performance adjudication defeats this confined claim despite the district court's added prejudice error. Genuine development allegations, their procedural treatment and other unreviewed sentence grounds remain distinct.

## Separate Writings

Justice Stevens joins the correct statutory and prejudice framework but dissents from the applications and judgment. He would affirm the Cronic-based sentence-only conditional writ. In his view, the combined failure to present an individualized case for life and answer the prosecution's request for death eliminated the relevant capital-sentencing function. He acknowledges the mercy opening, references to existing experts, favorable military cross-examination and protective objections; he considers them insufficient to constitute meaningful adversarial testing in the proceeding as a whole. His rule is not that every omitted witness or waived closing is structural, and he makes no finding that later illness establishes trial incapacity.

If Cronic is rejected, Stevens would also leave the ordinary tactical claim open on remand. He would require examination of the asserted legal misunderstandings, the performance ground's independence from the erroneous prejudice inquiry and its relationship to the disputed restrictions on mitigation development. Pritchett's limited support for expecting appellate correction does not, in his view, excuse surrender of a meaningful penalty defense. He accepts the State's independent-ground argument as substantial but would not foreclose ordinary appellate review on this record. He finds neither Strickland deficiency nor actual prejudice as an alternative present holding. His primary remedy preserves the existing 180-day structure for a new lawful penalty proceeding; the convictions and unused alternative sentence grounds are unaffected.

## Procedure After Action

The Sixth Circuit must proceed with the independently pending, legally available sentencing matters, excluding the decided available-mitigation and closing-argument claim. Investigation/development default, the asserted state-ground objections and cause, and other pretermitted sentencing grounds retain their own requirements. No new trial evidence, cause finding, hearing or funding entitlement follows from this remand.

## Source Notes

535 U.S. 685; No. 01-400; 243 F.3d 961 (6th Cir. 2001); 28 U.S.C. §2254; Cone v. State, 747 S.W.2d 353 (Tenn. Crim. App. 1987); Cozzolino v. State, 584 S.W.2d 765; State v. Pritchett, 621 S.W.2d 127; Cone v. State, 665 S.W.2d 87. The 1986 postconviction evidence concerning counsel does not establish incapacity at the 1982 trial.

<a id="Federal-Maritime-Commission-v-South-Carolina-State-Ports-Authority-Merits-2002-05-28"></a>

# Federal Maritime Commission v. South Carolina State Ports Authority; No. 01-46; 535 U.S. 743. — 2002-05-28

[Authoritative Record](../records/Federal_Maritime_Commission_v_South_Carolina_State_Ports_Authority_Merits_2002-05-28.md)

## Event

Federal Maritime Commission v. South Carolina State Ports Authority, No. 01-46, 535 U.S. 743. Merits decision, May 28, 2002. Argued February 25, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Fourth Circuit.

The question is whether state sovereign immunity prevents the Commission from adjudicating a private Shipping Act complaint against a state-operated port. South Carolina Maritime Services sought berths for the M/V Tropic Sea on five occasions and alleges discriminatory application of the port's policy against vessels principally devoted to gambling, compared with Carnival vessels offering gambling. It seeks reparations, cessation of the alleged discrimination and Commission pursuit of interim judicial relief. The port's state-arm status is uncontested. The ALJ dismissed on immunity grounds; the Commission reviewed that ruling on its own motion and reversed, although the complainant had not appealed. The Fourth Circuit directed dismissal. The United States supports reversal as a respondent.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at submission or argument and decision. Nine participate; the quorum is six and a majority is five.

## Public Action

The Court issues its signed merits decision.

## Judgment & Remedy

The Fourth Circuit's judgment is reversed and the case remanded for the Commission to adjudicate the complaint, 5–4. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Different disposition: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would vacate and remand for a distinct inquiry into unmistakable statutory authorization under the governing Article I rule.

The controlling disposition permits adjudication of the requested administrative reparations and prospective compliance relief under their actual statutory conditions. It orders no berth, reparation, fee award or injunction. A later independent judicial action retains its own authority, immunity and remedy requirements.

## Opinion Topology

STEVENS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and SOUTER, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined. KENNEDY, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which O'CONNOR, SCALIA and THOMAS, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverse and remand for Commission adjudication | Complete opinion, including the administrative-forum rule and all statutory and later-proceeding limits |
| Dissenting opinion | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas | Would vacate and remand for the distinct authorization determination | Private-compulsory-adjudication immunity subject to actual legislative power; no consent, automatic prospective-only exception or final abrogation holding |


## Holdings

### I. State immunity from private judicial suit does not bar this Commission process

**Controlling proposition:** State immunity from private judicial suit does not prevent the Federal Maritime Commission from administering the Shipping Act through this statutory complaint adjudication against a state-operated port. Private initiation, a request for personal reparations and court-like safeguards do not transform the Commission's determination of the regulatory controversy into an independent private judicial suit.

**Authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, including the author in that five-Justice coalition. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reject this tribunal rule and propose a different remand. Shared opposition to the existing final dismissal is not agreement on the controlling rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Commission applies the maritime statute, reviews the ALJ and issues its own administrative order through the enacted scheme. Congress's use of complaints and adjudicative safeguards is a method of national regulatory administration. Compulsory answer, discovery, hearing and potential individual relief impose real burdens; the Court does not deny them or rely merely on an executive label. Their presence does not change the actual federal statutory function into the private court action to which the asserted immunity attaches.

California establishes public-terminal coverage and the particular corrective regulatory power it sustained, not an answer to this distinct immunity question. Seminole's judicial-function holding concerned a court's conclusive determination; it does not classify every agency determination of public obligations as a private judicial suit. Waffle House recognizes an agency's independent statutory role, but this complaint remains privately initiated. The Commission's own-motion review did not convert it into a government-filed action.

The absence of immediate execution is not alone decisive, and the Court does not suppose that an eventual order is either unenforceable or automatically collectible. Those questions belong to their actual later proceedings. The holding concerns this Commission process, not every administrative tribunal. Because the immunity asserted does not bar this process, a separate Shipping Act abrogation determination is unnecessary.

**Precedent treatment:** California v. United States, 320 U.S. 577 (1944), retains its public-terminal and corrective-order holdings without an assumed private initiator or implied immunity ruling. Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, March 27, 1996, retains its conclusive-judicial-function holding. EEOC v. Waffle House, Inc., January 15, 2002, supplies an agency-role distinction without converting this private complaint into the agency's own enforcement action.

### II. Neither state status nor commercial activity supplies the omitted legal steps

**Controlling proposition:** The port's ordinary commercial operations and its defensive challenge establish no consent to suit; the Court's administrative-forum holding rests on the Commission's statutory function, not constructive waiver. The existing rule permitting unmistakably authorized State exposure within valid Article I power remains controlling, but this decision finds no affirmative or negative Shipping Act abrogation rule.

**Authority:** The five-Justice Court opinion states these limits. All four dissenters likewise reject supplied consent and the categorical Article I incapacity premise, but their proposed authorization remand remains distinct from the Court's agency-boundary ground.

**Controlling explanation:** College Savings Bank rejects fictional consent from ordinary commerce, and Lapides concerns actual authorized voluntary removal, an act not supplied here. Florida Prepaid and Garrett permit the separate inquiry into unmistakable authorization and enumerated power; Alden rejects the categorical state-forum barrier to its expressly authorized remedy. The Fourth Circuit's categorical Article I premise therefore cannot govern. Substantive coverage, authority for a particular private proceeding and actual waiver remain different questions. The Court need not decide the particular Shipping Act authorization alternative because its tribunal holding permits the complaint to proceed. Nor does Verizon Maryland create a connected officer defendant in this administrative complaint. Both requested forms of administrative relief may be adjudicated on the controlling institutional ground; no prospective-only immunity exception is necessary.

**Precedent treatment:** Florida Prepaid, June 23, 1999, Alden v. Maine, June 23, 1999, and Board of Trustees v. Garrett, February 21, 2001, retain their actual legislative-power and enforcement holdings. College Savings Bank, June 23, 1999, rejects constructive commercial consent. Lapides v. Board of Regents, May 13, 2002, remains confined to authorized removal; Verizon Maryland, May 20, 2002, preserves the distinct connected-officer route.

### III. Administrative adjudication and later enforcement remain separate

**Controlling proposition:** The Commission may adjudicate the complaint's requests under the Shipping Act's applicable coverage, violation and remedy provisions, without an immunity-based severance of reparations from prospective compliance. Any later judicial enforcement, collection or interim injunction must independently satisfy its own statutory, jurisdictional and immunity requirements; this decision establishes no substantive violation or award.

**Authority:** The five-Justice Court opinion controls the administrative remand. The four dissenters share the later-proceeding limits but would first require the separate authorization determination before the State may be compelled to defend this complaint.

**Controlling explanation:** Commission investigation begun on its own initiative is distinct from own-motion review of the ALJ in a private case. The Act also separates governmental or authorized private judicial enforcement of a nonreparation order, private judicial collection of reparations, Commission interim-injunction litigation and the complainant's separate interim judicial route. The Attorney General's nonreparation enforcement role does not authorize collection of the private reparation award. A Commission finding's specified evidentiary force does not automatically establish judicial jurisdiction or defeat immunity. No penalty for disobedience is before the Court.

Ordinary adjudication must apply the operative enactment to the particular conduct and requested relief. The 1998 amendments generally took effect May 1, 1999, while preserving suits filed before that date and suits concerning earlier conduct filed within one year afterward. The earlier enactment and amendments must remain distinct where coverage or an enhanced reparation depends on them. The Commission must determine actual carrier and terminal predicates, the discrimination allegation and lawful relief, with ordinary administrative review. The Court leaves immunity from a later independent private collection action outside this question, as well as different proceedings permitting full new judicial review without required prior participation.

**Precedent treatment:** California's public-terminal construction informs coverage without deciding these voyage-specific predicates or private enforcement. College Savings Bank and the governing legislative-authorization cases retain their work in a later proceeding to which their conditions apply.

## Precedent Treatment

The decision adds the confined rule for this Commission adjudication. California preserves public-terminal coverage and the particular correction it sustained, without an assumed initiating instrument or private-immunity holding. Seminole's judicial-function rule does not convert every agency adjudication into a private suit. Waffle House preserves the distinction between an agency's own action and this private complaint. Florida Prepaid, Alden and Garrett retain the valid-Article-I and particular-enforcement rules; no categorical incapacity returns. College Savings Bank rejects commerce-based consent. Lapides and Verizon Maryland retain their actual-removal and connected-officer conditions. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

A state-operated port cannot demand dismissal of this Shipping Act administrative complaint merely because a private complainant initiated it and seeks reparations. Both monetary and prospective requests may receive statutory adjudication; the agency's forum does not itself decide coverage, violation or relief. This is a Commission-process rule, not universal commercial waiver, a new private judicial cause of action or a ruling on later collection immunity.

## Separate Writings

Justice Kennedy, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas, dissents from the agency-boundary holding and reversal. The four would vacate and remand. Compulsory pleadings, response, discovery, hearing and adjudication of personal liability, in their view, impose the private-suit burden from which immunity protects a State. Commission supervision, an executive label and lack of immediate execution do not remove that burden or the eventual order's evidentiary consequences. They would not distinguish the cease-and-desist request automatically from reparations merely by calling it prospective; no officer defendant is created by the complaint.

They nevertheless reject the Fourth Circuit's complete dismissal rationale because the governing Florida Prepaid and Garrett rule permits unmistakably authorized State exposure under valid Article I power. They retain their original structural objection while applying that controlling rule. Their remand would identify the operative enactment, the particular complaint and remedy, and the statutory authorization before final disposition. Public-terminal coverage, the word person and commerce power alone neither establish nor defeat the necessary clarity; no single required verbal formula or present abrogation answer follows. California did not decide this private-immunity question. Actual government investigation and enforcement, proper connected-officer relief and later collection remain separate. Their proposed remand removes the dismissal direction but does not yet authorize ordinary merits adjudication on the Court's ground.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Fourth Circuit for Commission adjudication of the private complaint under the operative Shipping Act. The port retains coverage, merits and remedy defenses. Statutory version and transition questions must use the actual conduct and filing dates where material. Any subsequent judicial enforcement or collection presents its own conditions; this judgment orders none.

## Source Notes

535 U.S. 743; No. 01-46; 243 F.3d 165 (4th Cir. 2001); Shipping Act of 1984, Pub. L. 98-237, §§3, 10–14, 98 Stat. 67; Ocean Shipping Reform Act of 1998, Pub. L. 105-258, §§2, 102, 109–110, 117, 112 Stat. 1902; California v. United States, 320 U.S. 577. State-arm status is uncontested. No discrimination finding or completed monetary award is before the Court.

---

<a id="Festo-Corp-v-Shoketsu-Kinzoku-Kogyo-Kabushiki-Co-Merits-2002-05-28"></a>

# Festo Corp. v. Shoketsu Kinzoku Kogyo Kabushiki Co.; No. 00-1543; 535 U.S. 722. — 2002-05-28

[Authoritative Record](../records/Festo_Corp_v_Shoketsu_Kinzoku_Kogyo_Kabushiki_Co_Merits_2002-05-28.md)

## Event

Festo Corp. v. Shoketsu Kinzoku Kogyo Kabushiki Co., No. 00-1543, 535 U.S. 722. Merits decision, May 28, 2002. Argued January 8, 2002. On writ of certiorari to the Federal Circuit.

The questions concern actual claim narrowing, patentability purpose and the extent of prosecution-history estoppel—the restriction against recovering subject matter surrendered to obtain a patent. The en banc Federal Circuit reversed the infringement judgment under a complete bar to equivalents for amended limitations. The Stoll and Carroll patents concern magnetic rodless cylinder assemblies. The disputed limitations concern sealing rings in each patent and magnetizable sleeve material in Stoll. SMC's designs use a single two-way ring and, for the sleeve, nonmagnetizable aluminum alloy. The issued claim boundaries, the reasons and effects of the amendments, and the remaining infringement questions require distinct treatment.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at submission or argument and decision. Nine participate; the quorum is six and a majority is five.

## Public Action

The Court issues its signed merits decision.

## Judgment & Remedy

The Federal Circuit's en banc judgment is vacated and the case remanded, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Stoll ring restriction, Stoll magnetizable-sleeve restriction and Carroll ring restriction are qualifying patentability-related narrowings. The court of appeals must apply the rebuttable scope-of-surrender rule at those established triggers, subject to properly presented preservation objections, and decide the remaining all-elements and infringement issues in ordinary sequence. The accepted amendments and those trigger determinations are not returned for rediscovery or redetermination. Neither infringement judgment nor damages are automatically reinstated; no new evidence, hearing or repeated evidentiary opportunity is guaranteed.

## Opinion Topology

GINSBURG, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SCALIA, KENNEDY, SOUTER, THOMAS and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer | Unanimous vacatur and corrected-scope remand | All controlling propositions, three established trigger applications, rebuttable scope inquiry and remaining procedural/infringement conditions |


## Holdings

### I. Actual narrowing and patentability purpose are separate requirements

**Controlling proposition:** An amendment that actually narrows a claim to obtain the patent by satisfying a patentability requirement can create prosecution-history estoppel, including narrowing for a disclosure requirement. Actual narrowing is determined from the claim's legally operative scope before and after amendment in its patent and prosecution context; a voluntary change receives the same substantive inquiry, while a cosmetic or merely explanatory change is not automatically a surrender.

**Authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court is joined in full by the other eight participating Justices at the propositions and remand stated here. Existing Warner-Jenkinson propositions not reconsidered remain governed by that decision's own authority.

**Controlling explanation:** Warner-Jenkinson preserves nonliteral protection while enforcing actual claim limitations and prosecution surrender. Exhibit Supply protects what the applicant relinquished to obtain the patent. Neither confines patentability to novelty or prior-art avoidance. A substantive restriction addressing disclosure can therefore matter; describing an objection as formal does not immunize the ensuing narrowing. Conversely, a §112 rejection or changed words do not establish the purpose and legal scope of each resulting limitation.

Moving a restriction from a dependent claim into an independent claim can narrow that independent claim, notwithstanding the restriction's earlier presence elsewhere. The Court rejects the contrary categorical implication. A feature also need not be separately novel or demanded word-for-word by the examiner to contribute to a voluntary patentability narrowing. These conclusions give SMC's combination-purpose argument real force without treating the overall prosecution response as a conclusive answer for every limitation.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., March 3, 1997, is applied to actual limitation-specific surrender. Exhibit Supply Co. v. Ace Patents Corp., 315 U.S. 126 (1942), protects the actual concession without establishing an irrebuttable bar to all equivalents.

### II. The existing purpose-explanation burden remains distinct

**Controlling proposition:** When required narrowing lacks a disclosed legally sufficient reason, the patentee seeking an equivalent bears the explanatory burden. After fair consideration, an inadequate explanation permits the rebuttable presumption of a patentability-related reason and estoppel at the limitation; a supported nonpatentability explanation must be considered, including in the context of an older prosecution record.

**Controlling explanation:** Warner-Jenkinson already assigns this burden to the applicant, who chose the narrowed claim. It does not demand nonexistent contemporaneous formulations or turn historical silence into misconduct. Nor does it guarantee a hearing, reopening or another opportunity to discharge an existing burden. The Court adds no automatic purpose presumption for every voluntary textual change.

The written Stoll §112 response is established, but does not alone show that both the ring and sleeve terms had one purpose. Carroll's replacement combination was offered against the German reference, but the particular ring amendment still requires analysis. An attorney-error account cannot erase the issued sleeve restriction, although a supported nonpatentability explanation must receive its proper consideration. SMC's characterization of an effective concession and the district court's earlier description of the sleeve's reason as a mystery are not a stipulation that the burden has failed. The accepted record permits the three trigger determinations stated below; an admission is not their prerequisite.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson's explanation burden and older-record qualification are applied unchanged; no subjective bad-faith condition or automatic second evidentiary opportunity is added.

### III. Qualifying narrowing presumptively surrenders the asserted equivalent, subject to concrete rebuttal

**Controlling proposition:** Once patentability-related narrowing is established, the patentee presumptively surrendered the asserted equivalent at the affected limitation. The patentee may rebut with a concrete, objectively supported basis, anchored in the prosecution, showing that the actual relinquishment did not encompass that variation—for example, that the equivalent was not reasonably foreseeable at the relevant prosecution stage, that the amendment's rationale related only tangentially to it, or that it could not reasonably have been described within the claim at that time.

**Controlling explanation:** A complete bar sacrifices all nonliteral protection beyond the actual concession. Unrestricted flexibility, however, would deny competitors the public boundary produced by amendment. The rebuttable presumption gives each interest legal effect. The accused infringer need not first prove that this very equivalent was surrendered; that would collapse the trigger and scope inquiries. The patentee must establish the concrete reason the variation remains outside the surrender, judged at the amendment rather than through an uncommunicated intention to retain broad rights. The stated grounds are not automatic safe harbors, a closed mechanical list or an unstructured fairness balance.

Objective grounding in the prosecution does not require a universal exclusion of reliable technical or explanatory evidence outside the contemporaneous file. Such evidence may illuminate the claim, prosecution and reason for amendment, particularly in an older record. It cannot contradict a clear public concession, delete a claim term or substitute the inventor's private wishes for the grant. Ordinary procedure governs a proffer's admissibility and any further evidentiary opportunity. Markman permits useful technical explanation while leaving public claim meaning to the court; Warner-Jenkinson requires fair treatment of supported explanations without automatic reopening.

Successful rebuttal removes only this estoppel barrier; technical equivalence and infringement still require proof. Failed rebuttal forecloses that equivalent at the amended limitation. This scope presumption is separate from the existing presumption about an unexplained amendment's purpose.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson is developed through the explicit rebuttable scope presumption while its purpose burden remains intact. Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., April 23, 1996, preserves judicial claim construction and useful technical evidence without dictating every appellate standard or a special hearing. Graver Tank & Manufacturing Co. v. Linde Air Products Co., 339 U.S. 605 (1950), retains protection against insubstantial substitutions within the legal claim boundaries.

### IV-A. The Stoll ring amendment is qualifying patentability narrowing

**Controlling proposition:** Incorporating the pair-of-rings restriction into Stoll's replacement independent claim narrowed that claim and served a patentability purpose in the response to the §112 rejection. Festo's account that the old sealing means already distinguished the references does not defeat that trigger, although it may bear on whether the amendment's rationale encompassed the single-ring equivalent under the separate scope inquiry.

**Controlling explanation:** The original independent claim's general sealing means at each end and the replacement's specific ring structure are not interchangeable. The restriction's earlier presence in a dependent claim did not give the old independent claim the same limit. This comparison adopts no general means-plus-function construction and decides no technical equivalence.

The replacement sought allowance after the disclosure rejection, requested reconsideration under §112 and addressed impurity-prevention structure in discussing the references. Even accepting that the older claim already distinguished those references, the applicant chose a more specific description to answer the statutory objection. That is a patentability reason; the examiner need not dictate the exact words. It is unnecessary to decide SMC's additional contention that the rings were independently indispensable to avoiding the art. The asserted earlier distinction remains potentially relevant to the actual extent of surrender, but does not itself rebut the scope presumption.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson's actual-narrowing and patentability rule is applied to this specific claim and response; its preserved equivalents inquiry supplies the separate work on remand.

### IV-B. The Stoll sleeve amendment is qualifying patentability narrowing

**Controlling proposition:** Adding magnetizable sleeve material to Stoll's replacement independent claim narrowed that claim in the documented patentability response. The earlier proffer of inventor nonintention, lack of independent necessity and attorney mistake does not establish a supported nonpatentability purpose for the attorney's actual submission or permit deletion of the issued restriction; the scope presumption applies to the nonmagnetizable-sleeve equivalent, subject to Festo's separate rebuttal burden.

**Controlling explanation:** The original independent claim lacked this material restriction. The applicant incorporated it from a dependent claim while recasting the rejected claim to obtain allowance. Neither a separate examiner demand nor independent necessity of magnetizability is a condition of the narrowing's legal effect. The sleeve remains distinct from the cylinder tube and the ring limitations.

The Court fairly considers the earlier account that the inventor neither intended nor authorized the restriction and that it was unnecessary. Even accepting the asserted absence of inventor intent, the proffer gives no supported nonpatentability reason for the prosecuting attorney's actual selection; it instead requests that the claim be read without the chosen words. A genuinely supported nonpatentability explanation can matter, including when counsel made the amendment. The asserted mistake here does not displace the objective submission or remove the literal term. No finding of false testimony or disobedient attorney intent follows.

This conclusion rests on the actual response and the legal insufficiency of the contrary grounds. It does not rest on a supposed concession, the district judge's description of the reason as a mystery, or historical silence treated as misconduct. It adds no presumption that every voluntary edit is patentability-related and demands no nonexistent contemporary formula. Rejecting literal deletion still does not conclusively extinguish every sleeve equivalent; that remains the separate scope inquiry.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson's fair-consideration and public-claim rules are applied together. Its older-record qualification neither makes an explanation automatically sufficient nor guarantees another evidentiary opportunity.

### IV-C. The Carroll ring amendment is qualifying patentability narrowing

**Controlling proposition:** Replacing Carroll's former independent claim with claim 9's express pair-of-rings limitation narrowed the claim as part of the combination submitted to distinguish the German reference and preserve patentability. The feature's alleged presence in the reference and the emphasis on other features do not defeat that trigger, although those particulars may bear on the scope of surrender of the single-ring equivalent.

**Controlling explanation:** Disclosure of rings in the specification did not itself impose the new limitation on the former independent claim. The Court identifies this ring restriction rather than substituting another component's narrowing. The applicant included it in the chosen replacement combination offered against the reference. Independent novelty, indispensability and a word-for-word examiner demand are not prerequisites to the legal effect of that choice.

The verified emphasis on magnets, end members and cushions may show a tangential relationship between the amendment's rationale and the asserted substitute; Festo must establish the relevant scope showing. Its acknowledgment of some unrelated abandonment neither concedes surrender of this equivalent nor conclusively rebuts the presumption. The Court applies the rule to this accepted ring addition and documented response, without declaring that every element of every amended combination necessarily has one purpose. Stoll's disclosure rejection and sleeve explanation are not attributed to Carroll.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson's limitation-specific surrender rule governs the trigger, while the separate scope presumption preserves the legal relevance of the particular amendment rationale to the accused substitute.

### V. Preservation and the all-elements inquiry retain their own work

**Controlling proposition:** A dispositive preservation ruling must identify the actual defense, procedural act and governing rule for the particular patent and stage. The separate requirement that every material claim element be satisfied remains operative; vacating the complete-bar judgment decides neither that objection nor the legally available infringement and remedy questions.

**Controlling explanation:** The Stoll trial exchanges include counsel's immediate disagreement with an abandonment characterization. They establish neither a general surrender of estoppel nor a Carroll-specific waiver. The separate report that Carroll estoppel was not raised at summary judgment retains its attributed procedural scope. The court of appeals must address a properly presented objection under the applicable rule, without assuming an unestablished procedural omission. It must also address the all-elements objection that the en banc court left unresolved. Legal boundaries come first; genuine remaining infringement facts retain ordinary factfinder treatment. Further district-court work follows only when necessary under the corrected rule and ordinary procedure.

**Precedent treatment:** Warner-Jenkinson's element-specific equivalents rule remains controlling; overall similarity cannot erase a material limitation. Its existing no-bad-intent rule is not reconsidered, and retains that decision's eight-Justice authority.

## Precedent Treatment

Warner-Jenkinson's limitation-specific protection, purpose-explanation burden and contextual older-record qualification remain. This decision adds the rebuttable scope-of-surrender presumption after a qualifying narrowing, without merging the two burdens. Its existing no-bad-intent holding remains at its original authority. Graver Tank preserves nonliteral protection for insubstantial differences within the claim boundaries, and Exhibit Supply protects actual prosecution surrender. Markman preserves judicial construction, including embedded technical-usage questions and useful explanatory evidence, while leaving external infringement facts and unaddressed appellate standards in their ordinary places. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

Narrowing for any patentability requirement, including disclosure, may trigger estoppel. The law now expressly presumes surrender of the asserted equivalent after that trigger, while permitting concrete objective rebuttal directed to what the amendment reasonably relinquished. Required-narrowing explanation and scope rebuttal are separate burdens. Every material limitation remains operative; neither rejection of a complete bar nor successful estoppel rebuttal establishes infringement. Older records receive fair treatment without automatic evidentiary reopening. The three disputed amendments meet the trigger; only particular-equivalent scope rebuttal and the surviving ordinary procedural and infringement applications return for decision.

## Separate Writings



## Procedure After Action

The case returns first to the Federal Circuit for Festo's particular-equivalent rebuttal at the three established narrowing triggers, properly presented preservation objections and remaining all-elements and infringement questions. The existing legal triggers are not reopened. Further district-court proceedings occur only as required by the corrected scope rule and ordinary procedure. No patent invalidation, infringement judgment, damages award, new hearing or evidentiary reopening is ordered.

## Source Notes

535 U.S. 722; No. 00-1543; 234 F.3d 558 (Fed. Cir. 2000) (en banc); 35 U.S.C. §112; Joint Appendix I-40–44, I-53–56, I-72–80, I-136–141; II-24–25, II-30–31, II-36–40, II-180–189. The accepted written amendments and the parties' qualified explanations remain distinct from final legal conclusions about narrowing, purpose or surrender.
